# Adam Elsheimer: Study Notes

**Site:** Adam Elsheimer (baptised 18 March 1578, Frankfurt am Main - 11 December 1610, Rome), German painter in Rome; maker of tiny oil-on-copper cabinet pictures whose nocturnes, spread by engraving, seeded the Dutch moonlight landscape. His Flight into Egypt (c. 1609, Munich), 31 x 41 cm, is thought to be the first naturalistic night sky in paint, Milky Way included; Hendrick Goudt engraved it in 1613 and carried the nocturne into the Dutch print market a generation before Aert van der Neer's first moonlight of 1644. He died aged 32, in poverty, his pictures already coveted by princes and hidden in their cabinets. His whole surviving oeuvre is about thirty to forty small pictures, most of them on copper, most of them no bigger than a hand.

**Doorway:** Hop from the Aert van der Neer study: the German in Rome whose Flight into Egypt, engraved by Hendrick Goudt in 1613, carried the nocturne into the Dutch print market a generation before Aert's first moonlight of 1644. The link is print-mediated, NOT contact: Elsheimer died in Rome in December 1610, nineteen years before Aert is first recorded as a painter (identified as a painter in his 1629 marriage licence) and thirty-four years before Aert's first Moonlight Scene of 1644. The doorway is the print that taught a whole school to paint the night -- and this study is the copper, the four lights, and the sky that the print carried.

One-hundred-and-seventy-third doorway check in a row. D1 "Adam Elsheimer (1578-1610)" -- HOLDS in substance and in letter (baptised 18 March 1578, Frankfurt; died 11 December 1610, Rome, aged 32; EN Wikipedia read end to end in legs 1 and 3, 229 lines each; leg 1 section 1; leg 3 C1). D2 "the German in Rome who taught the Dutch to paint by moonlight" -- HOLDS in substance and FAILS in the letter ("German in Rome" holds -- Wikipedia lead; but "taught" implies direct pedagogy and there was none: Elsheimer never taught a Dutch painter in person, his only documented pupil was the Dutch engraver Goudt in Rome; the mechanism is print-mediated genre seeding -- the NGA: Dutch interest in moonlit landscapes "can be traced to engravings made by Hendrik Goudt after paintings by Adam Elsheimer"; the same NGA article cautions that Aert "developed his style rather independently" -- see C623). D3 "His Flight into Egypt (c. 1609, Munich) is the first naturalistic night sky in paint, Milky Way included" -- HOLDS in substance and FAILS in the letter (the picture is "thought to be the first naturalistic rendering of the night sky in Renaissance art" -- Wikipedia Flight article, citing Howard 1992; the National Gallery of Ireland calls it "the first ever recording of the Milky Way in paint"; but the claim is a consensus "thought to be", and its literal accuracy is contested -- Howard and Longair 2011 argue the sky could never have been observed as painted and the picture is "more a religious iconographic text than an accurate representation of the sky" -- see C624). ## The life

Adam Elsheimer was baptised on 18 March 1578 in Frankfurt am Main, one of ten children of a master tailor, and died in Rome on 11 December 1610, aged 32. His father's house stood a few metres from the church where Albrecht Durer's Heller Altarpiece was then displayed; the house survived until destroyed by Allied bombs in 1944. He was apprenticed to the Frankfurt artist Philipp Uffenbach, who specialised in large altarpieces. He probably visited Strasbourg in 1596. At twenty he travelled to Italy via Munich, where he is documented in 1598.

His stay in Venice, c. 1598-1600, is undocumented, but the influence on his style is clear. He probably worked as an assistant to Johann Rottenhammer, a German already living in Italy and the first German painter to specialise in cabinet paintings; Elsheimer owned some of Rottenhammer's drawings. The National Gallery says he worked in Venice "for about two years" and that The Baptism of Christ was apparently painted there. Works believed to come from Venice include The Baptism of Christ (National Gallery, London) and The Holy Family (Gemaldegalerie, Berlin), showing the influence of Tintoretto and Paolo Veronese as well as Rottenhammer. His earliest small paintings on copper seem to date from before he arrived in Italy.

In early 1600 he arrived in Rome and quickly made friends with contacts of Rottenhammer, notably Giovanni Faber, a papal doctor, botanist and collector originally from Bamberg, curator of the Vatican Botanical Garden and a member of the Accademia dei Lincei, the small intellectual coterie founded in 1603 and mainly concerned with the natural sciences. Another Rottenhammer friend was the Flemish landscape painter Paul Bril, already established in Rome, who was (with Faber) a witness at Elsheimer's marriage, painted a picture together with him (now Chatsworth House), and was owed money by him at his death; like Faber, Bril was a long-term resident in Rome who had converted from Lutheranism to Catholicism, as Elsheimer did later. The National Gallery: "From 1600 until his death Elsheimer lived and worked in Rome, where Rubens struck up a friendship with him."

Rubens was in Rome in 1601 and became another friend, later reproaching Elsheimer for not producing more work. He knew David Teniers the Elder, recently Rubens' pupil, and there is evidence that they lodged together. The Kimbell page says he was "part of a circle of northerners in Rome that also included Rubens." Tobias and the Angel (the "small" Tobias, now at Frankfurt) was especially well received because of its new conception of landscape; it was engraved by Count Hendrick Goudt and as a result published across Europe. In 1604 Karel van Mander, a Dutchman recently returned from Rome, published his Schilder-Boeck, which praised Elsheimer's work and described him as slow-working and making few drawings; he also spent much time in churches, studying the works of the masters. Other writers mention his exceptional visual memory, his melancholy and his kind-heartedness.

His association with Goudt, who lodged and trained with him for several years, was difficult. Elsheimer seems to have borrowed money from Goudt, which according to one account resulted in his brief incarceration in a debtor's prison -- a single-account claim, unconfirmed by any other source read (leg 1 F5). After Elsheimer's early death in 1610 in Rome, Goudt owned several of his pictures. Goudt made seven engravings of Elsheimer's paintings -- or five in one series, the sources disagree (see C625) -- which were crucial in spreading his influence, as very few of his paintings were viewable even by artists, since as cabinet paintings they were mostly kept in small and very private rooms. The Met object page for Goudt's Flight into Egypt says Goudt "lived in Elsheimer's house in Rome from about 1604 to 1610. He may have been the German painter's student but was also an avid patron."

The Frankfurt Altarpiece of the Holy Cross (also called the Altarpiece of the Exaltation of the True Cross) dates to about 1603-1605. The Stadel calls it "Elsheimer's greatest work", a seven-part domestic altarpiece: the central panel shows the exaltation of the Cross and the six scenes around it recount the legend of the finding of the Cross by St Helena and its later return to Jerusalem by Emperor Heraclius. It was documented but presumed lost; between 1950 and 1981 the Stadel reunited its parts, and the modern frame was reconstructed after sketches made in 1612.

In 1606 he married Carola Antonia Stuarda da Francoforte, of Scottish ancestry and a fellow Frankfurter, recent widow of the artist Nicolas de Breul (born in Verdun); in 1609 they had a son. The son was not mentioned in a census a year later, possibly (Klessmann says optimistically) because he had been put out to a wet-nurse; the child's fate is unknown. After Elsheimer's death she remarried an Italian artist, Ascanio Quercia, within a year. In 1606 he was admitted to the Accademia di San Luca, the Roman painters' guild, giving them a self-portrait, his only portrait and his only painting on canvas, now in the Uffizi. By 1608 (possibly 1606) he converted to Catholicism.

His patron, per the Wikipedia infobox, was Cardinal Francesco Maria del Monte. The del Monte article states "Del Monte was a patron of German painter Adam Elsheimer", citing Deborah Howard 1992. No source read supports Hans Fugger as a patron of Elsheimer; Fugger died in 1598, the year Elsheimer left for Italy, and the Fugger patronage documented in the sources read concerns earlier artists (leg 1 F12). Do not list Fugger as a patron.

In 1609 he painted The Flight into Egypt (Alte Pinakothek, Munich), oil on copper, 31 cm x 41 cm, inscribed on the back "Adam Elsheimer fecit Romae 1609". It was hanging in his bedroom in Rome at his death. He died on 11 December 1610, aged 32, and is buried at San Lorenzo in Lucina, Rome. "In spite of his fame and talents, he appears to have both lived and died in difficult financial circumstances" (Wikipedia). The Kimbell page: "He died in poverty, although his works were coveted."

Afterlife: a letter dated 14 January 1611 from Rubens to Johann Faber discusses the extraordinary price of 300 scudi demanded by the widow for The Flight into Egypt; the painting eventually passed to Hendrick Goudt, who took it to Utrecht. Goudt engraved the Flight in 1613. In a letter after his death Rubens wrote of Elsheimer: "he had no equal in small figures, landscapes, and in many other subjects. ...one could have expected things from him that one has never seen before and never will see." Rubens owned at least four of his works. Soon after his death he became very popular with English collectors, notably King Charles I, the Earl of Arundel and George Villiers, 1st Duke of Buckingham, and over half his paintings have been in English collections at some time, nearly one third still in the UK. In 2006 an exhibition at the Stadel, Frankfurt, then Edinburgh and Dulwich Picture Gallery in London, reunited almost all of his oeuvre; the Edinburgh showing displayed over 30 known paintings, mostly on copper, and each ticket came with a loaned magnifying glass "the better to explore these brilliant miniaturist works."

## M1. Copper ground as light amplifier

What it IS: oil paint laid in thin layers on a smooth, nonporous copper plate (sometimes silvered), so the ground itself returns light through the paint instead of absorbing it. "As the work is painted on a smooth nonporous copper surface, the luminosity of the paint is intensified, allowing details to be sharpened and the colours to sparkle out of the darkness" (Philip McCouat, Journal of Art in Society). The earlier Flight into Egypt (Kimbell, c. 1605) is oil on silvered copper and only 9.8 cm high; silvering the copper adds a further luminosity. How it shows: lit passages -- moon, torch flame, campfire, moon-path on water -- glow from inside the dark; fine detail stays crisp at a few centimetres of scale; darks stay deep without going muddy. -- McCouat; Dobrzynski; Wikipedia (silvered copper, Kimbell Flight).

**Takeaway:** the plate is the lamp. A piece built on this mechanism must make its light come from under the paint -- a reflective ground under thin darks -- so the bright passages glow instead of sitting on top.

## M2. Multiple graded light sources in one frame

What it IS: up to five distinct lights of different colour, temperature and strength (moon, torch, campfire, starlight, reflected water-light), each assigned its own patch of the picture and graded gently into the dark rather than slammed against it, unlike Caravaggio's single hard source. "He often uses as many as five different sources of light. He graduates the light relatively gently, with the less well-lit parts of the composition often containing important parts of it" (Wikipedia). His lighting effects "were very subtle, and very different from those of Caravaggio". The Flight into Egypt (Munich) has four sources of light: the Moon, accurately depicted and reflected off the calm water; a fire near the shepherds at left; at the centre Joseph holds a torch that illuminates Mary and the infant riding an ass; and the stars. How it shows: the eye travels from light to light; no single spotlight dominates; important passages can sit in the dimmer zones. -- Wikipedia (five sources; gentle graduation; unlike Caravaggio); Wikipedia Flight article (four sources enumerated).

**Takeaway:** five lamps, none of them a spotlight. A piece built on this mechanism must run several lights of different temperatures at once, graded gently, and let the eye travel between them instead of fixing on one.

## M3. The observed night sky: stars as individual points, Milky Way as a river of points

What it IS: over a thousand individual stars painted dot by dot; the Milky Way rendered as a dense band of separate points rather than a smooth pale wash; the Moon shown with an irregular, cratered face about 1 cm across. "For the first time the Milky Way was shown resolved into myriads of individual stars" and features on the Moon's surface are carefully illustrated (Howard and Longair 2011). The relative positions of the stars in Ursa Major and Leo are accurately represented, and the brightest star of Leo, Regulus, is prominent. How it shows: the sky reads as a real, countable field rather than a black backdrop; the star-band becomes a compositional diagonal. -- McCouat ("over a thousand individual stars"); Howard and Longair 2011 (first resolution of the Milky Way into stars; Moon 1 cm).

**Takeaway:** the sky is counted, not painted. A piece built on this mechanism must build its night from discrete points -- hundreds or thousands of them -- so the Milky Way reads as a river of stars and the sky as a field you could count.

## M4. The diagonal sweep: Milky Way and tree-line in parallel

What it IS: a diagonal from top left to bottom right, made twice -- once by the Milky Way band in the sky, once by the dark tree-line below -- so the two diagonals echo and the eye is funnelled to the Holy Family at the centre. The heavily treed landscape behind is almost black, its outline forming a diagonal across the sky (Wikipedia, Flight article). How it shows: a vast sky that still points at a tiny group of figures; the picture recedes from foreground left to background right. -- McCouat; Wikipedia Flight article.

**Takeaway:** two diagonals, one job. A piece built on this mechanism must run the sky-band and the ground-line in parallel, so the whole vast picture points at one small group.

## M5. Story-within-landscape: tiny figures, dominant setting

What it IS: large foreground figures set inside a landscape that occupies most of the frame (in the Flight, both human scenes sit in the lower third while sky dominates). The landscape is the subject; the story is small inside it. "His combination of poetic landscape with large foreground figures gives the landscape a prominence that had rarely been seen since the Early Renaissance" (Wikipedia). "Both scenes reside in the lower third... truly a landscape" (Dobrzynski). McCouat: three clumps of trees mark three genre scenes -- pastoral idyll, family scene, moonlit landscape. How it shows: intimacy against immensity; the viewer reads the place first, the narrative second. -- Wikipedia (landscape prominence); Dobrzynski; McCouat.

**Takeaway:** the place is the picture. A piece built on this mechanism must put the story in the lower third and give the rest to landscape and sky, so the viewer finds the figures inside the world instead of the world behind the figures.

## M6. Low horizon, oblique recession

What it IS: a low horizon line with diagonals moving obliquely into the background instead of a centred, frontal recession. The Flight's composition, "a low horizon and diagonals moving obliquely into background, had a major influence on landscape painting in Italy and Northern Europe" (McCouat). How it shows: depth built sideways as well as backwards; the ground plane tilts and the picture feels entered from a corner. -- McCouat.

**Takeaway:** enter from the corner. A piece built on this mechanism must set the horizon low and let the diagonals run obliquely, so the depth is sideways as well as backwards.

## M7. Underplayed drama, moment of transformation

What it IS: the depicted instant is the quiet start of a change, not its climax; figures are short, stocky, unflamboyant, with Early Netherlandish rather than bella figura expressions. "His figures are relatively short and stocky, and reflect little of classical ideals. Their poses and gestures are unflamboyant, and their facial expressions resemble those in Early Netherlandish painting rather than the bella figura of most Italian Renaissance work" (Wikipedia). "His compositions tend to underplay the drama of the events they depict (in noticeable contrast to those of Rubens), but often show the start of moments of transformation" (Wikipedia). How it shows: stillness that implies motion to come; the viewer supplies the drama. -- Wikipedia.

**Takeaway:** the moment before, not the moment of. A piece built on this mechanism must stage the quiet start of a change -- unflamboyant figures, still poses -- and let the viewer supply the drama that follows.

## M8. Rare-subject selection

What it IS: choosing episodes nobody had painted (Philemon and Baucis, Mocking of Ceres, Apollo and Coronis, Il Contento) or an unusual moment of a familiar story (St Lawrence prepared for Martyrdom; the Flight at night). "He had a definite preference for choosing rare or original subjects, both for his mythological and religious paintings" (Wikipedia). Jupiter and Mercury in the house of Philemon and Baucis (c. 1608, Dresden) is based on an episode in Ovid "and had never been painted before". The Mocking of Ceres (Kingston), Apollo and Coronis (Liverpool) and Il Contento (Edinburgh) "were equally new". The Flight itself was the first depiction of that subject at night, although the biblical account (Matthew 2:14, flight by night) supports it. How it shows: a picture that cannot be read by habit; the viewer must look at the image rather than recall the standard version. -- Wikipedia; McCouat; Dobrzynski.

**Takeaway:** paint the unpainted episode. A piece built on this mechanism must take a subject nobody has used -- or the unpainted moment of a familiar one -- so the viewer has to look instead of remembering.

## M9. Miniature precision demanding close looking

What it IS: whole worlds painted at 10-40 cm, with detail (carpenter's tools, water gourd, broad-brimmed pilgrim hats, individual stars) that only resolves at inches' distance; the 2006 Edinburgh exhibition issued magnifying glasses with tickets. His perfectionism and an apparent tendency to depression resulted in a small total output despite the small size of all his pictures (Wikipedia). The Kimbell page: "According to contemporary accounts, Elsheimer worked slowly and thoughtfully, producing a relatively small number of finished works." How it shows: a private, lean-in viewing posture; the picture rewards slow inspection and hides its detail from a passing glance. -- Kimbell page (pilgrim attributes, carpenter's tools); Studio International (magnifying glasses); Wikipedia (small scale).

**Takeaway:** the picture is a secret. A piece built on this mechanism must hide its detail at arm's length and reward the lean-in -- small scale, dense incident, nothing legible from the doorway.

## M10. Light from below

What it IS: figures lit from underneath by a hand-held torch or a low fire, so faces and drapery are modelled from below rather than from above. A Museum of Fine Arts Houston bulletin notes the device of "lighting of the figures from below", which "produces an effect of spiritual incandescence and weightlessness." In the Flight, Joseph's torch illuminates Mary and the infant from below at the centre. How it shows: a weightless, incandescent quality; the holy figures seem lit by the world they walk through, not by heaven. -- MFAH bulletin (search snippet); Wikipedia Flight article (Joseph's torch illuminates Mary and the infant).

**Takeaway:** light from the ground up. A piece built on this mechanism must put its light low -- torch, fire, reflection -- and model its figures from underneath, so they glow instead of being lit.

## M11. The sky as iconographic text

What it IS: constellations placed for meaning, not observation -- Leo (kingship, Christ) centred over the Holy Family, Regulus ("little king") over the Christ child, the Great Bear (Diana, chastity, Mary) at the side, the Milky Way slanting like Jacob's Ladder, the path to heaven. Leo, placed centrally above the Holy Family, had since antiquity been associated with the Sun, kingship, strength and by extension Christ; Regulus, meaning "little king", had only recently been so named by Copernicus, and is placed centrally over the Christ Child; the Virgin Mary is traditionally associated with the Moon as a symbol of purity and with the stars; the Milky Way had symbolized the path to heaven since the Middle Ages (Howard and Longair 2011; Wikipedia, Flight article). McCouat adds: the Great Bear is associated with Diana, goddess of the moon, chastity and virginity, virtues also associated with Mary, and the downward slant of the Milky Way recalls its traditional guise as "Jacob's Ladder". How it shows: astronomy doubling as theology; the same stars are both a sky and a sentence. -- Howard and Longair 2011; McCouat; Wikipedia Flight article.

**Takeaway:** the stars are a sentence. A piece built on this mechanism must place its constellations by meaning, not by observation -- the sky as text the viewer can read, not a record of a night.

## M12. Print as the distribution system

What it IS: because the originals were tiny, private, and mostly hidden in cabinets, the work travelled as Goudt's engravings (1612-1613), which translated tonal night effects into black-and-white line that Dutch printmakers could copy. "Goudt made seven engravings of Elsheimer's paintings, which were crucial in spreading his influence, as very few of his paintings were viewable even by artists, since as cabinet paintings they were mostly kept in small and very private rooms" (Wikipedia). The Met: "Goudt's dark, rich night prints exercised a significant influence on Dutch printmakers of the Golden Age, including Hercules Segers and Rembrandt van Rijn." The NGA on Aert's Moonlit Landscape with Bridge: "Interest in moonlit landscapes in the Netherlands can be traced to engravings made by Hendrik Goudt (1585-1648) after paintings by Adam Elsheimer (1578-1610) in the second decade of the seventeenth century." How it shows: influence without access -- artists who never saw a copper panel learned the nocturne from paper. -- Wikipedia (seven engravings "crucial in spreading his influence"); Met page (Goudt's night prints influenced Segers and Rembrandt); NGA article (Dutch moonlit landscape traced to Goudt engravings).

**Takeaway:** the copy is the carrier. A piece built on this mechanism must work as a transmission object -- something that survives being copied, reduced, and reprinted, and still teaches the night to strangers who never saw the original.

## Works

Twenty documented works, ordered chronologically as far as sources allow. Dimensions follow the definition established in #533: VERIFIED means the figure appears on a museum or collection page read end to end; REPORTED-BUT-UNVERIFIED means a figure reported in a search result or secondary listing, with its source named; NOT VERIFIED means no reliable dimension figure was found in the material read. Tally: 3 verified, 15 reported-but-unverified, 2 not verified.

## W1 -- Rest on the Flight into Egypt, 1599

Oil on copper, 37 x 24 cm (REPORTED-BUT-UNVERIFIED -- wahooart listing; no museum page read end to end). Staatliche Museen zu Berlin (Gemaldegalerie). The earliest dated work in the list, painted in Venice or just before Italy. The Holy Family at rest on the journey: Mary and the child seated, Joseph standing by, in a wooded landscape. It is the daytime, restful counterpart to the two later Flight pictures (W12, W20): same family, same journey, but daylight and repose instead of night and flight. The 37 x 24 cm upright format is the small copper cabinet format Elsheimer never leaves.

## W2 -- The Baptism of Christ, c. 1599

Oil on copper, 28.1 x 21 cm (REPORTED-BUT-UNVERIFIED -- Griselda Pollock (ed.), Conceptual Odysseys (2007), list of illustrations, via search result; the National Gallery object page was not opened end to end). National Gallery, London, NG 3904. A Venice-period work, one of the two pictures Wikipedia names as showing the influence of Tintoretto, Veronese and Rottenhammer. Christ stands in the Jordan while John baptizes him, the river running through a wide landscape rather than a narrow devotional foreground; the turbaned oriental figure in the background reappears in the Saint Lawrence (W6), which the Foister 2024 catalogue uses to argue the Baptism predates it. The landscape already does half the work of the picture -- the Venetian lesson, carried into the cabinet format.

## W3 -- The Holy Family with the infant John the Baptist and angels, 1599/1600

Oil on copper, 37.8 x 24.4 cm (Wikidata); the Wikipedia caption gives 37.5 x 24 cm (REPORTED-BUT-UNVERIFIED -- neither figure comes from a page read end to end). Gemaldegalerie, Berlin. Date FLAG: 1599 (one Wikidata reference) vs 1600 (another). The second of the two Venice-period pictures named by Wikipedia, alongside the Baptism. The Holy Family with the infant John and angels in a landscape: the figures are large for Elsheimer, arranged close to the picture plane, and the patterned robe of the angel here resembles the gold damask robe of the turbaned man in the Saint Lawrence (Foister 2024). Venice in the handling -- warm colour, soft modelling -- but the small copper and the landscape setting are already his own.

## W4 -- St Paul on Malta, c. 1600

Oil on copper, 17.3 x 21.5 cm (REPORTED-BUT-UNVERIFIED -- Pollock, Conceptual Odysseys, illustration list, via search result). National Gallery, London, NG 3535. Paul shipwrecked on Malta (Acts 28), gathering sticks for a fire while a viper fastens on his hand, the islanders watching. At 17.3 x 21.5 cm it is one of the smallest pictures in the oeuvre, and the subject is already the Elsheimer formula: a biblical episode reduced to a handful of figures in a landscape that is doing the storytelling -- the shore, the fire, the onlookers. FLAG: the allpaintingsstore / wahooart listings wrongly call the medium "acrylic on canvas"; oil on copper is the medium given by the Pollock book list and by the National Gallery's own catalogue tradition.

## W5 -- Jacob's Dream, c. 1600

Oil on copper, 19.5 x 26.1 cm (REPORTED-BUT-UNVERIFIED -- artsdot / wikioo / wahooart listings agree on 19 x 26 cm; no Stadel page read end to end). Stadelsches Kunstinstitut, Frankfurt am Main. Jacob asleep with his head on a stone, the ladder to heaven with angels ascending and descending above him, rendered as a quiet nocturne: the dream is a matter of light in darkness rather than of architecture. Formerly in the private collection of Robert von Hirsch, 1883-1977, before entering the Stadel. One of the earliest Roman pictures, and already a night scene -- the nocturne is present from the start of the Rome decade, not a late invention.

## W6 -- Saint Lawrence prepared for Martyrdom, c. 1601-2

Oil on copper (panel less than 1 mm thick, slightly buckled, stuck to fibreboard; thin mid-grey priming with lead white, charcoal black and yellow earth; heat-bodied linseed oil medium), 26.8 x 20.9 cm (VERIFIED -- National Gallery online catalogue entry, Foister 2024, read end to end, main text lines 0-696). National Gallery, London, NG 1014 (Wynn Ellis bequest, 1876). Date FLAG: Andrews dated it c. 1600, Drost c. 1602-3, Weiszacker argued for the Venice period before Rome, Levey c. 1600, Klessmann close to the Stoning of Saint Stephen (1603-4). The Foister dating rests on the extent of the revisions and a slight disjunction between compositional elements, marking it as early Rome. Lawrence stands at left in a white robe, arms outspread, being disrobed before his martyrdom on the gridiron; a huge statue of a pagan god (Hercules, with club and lion skin) occupies nearly the whole right side; two youths stoke the gridiron fire at lower right; an angel hovers above Lawrence with the palm of martyrdom, pointing upward. The scene is set at night or dusk, the red glow of the fire set against the brilliance of the white-robed saint and angel -- a visual realization of Lawrence's words in the Golden Legend, "Everything is ablaze with light". The catalogue's technical notes record extensive reworking: the angel's arm and hand moved, the emperor moved down, a canopy added late over a continuous landscape, the red cloak painted over the gridiron, the turbaned man's robe and leg reposed repeatedly. The composition borrows from Durer's Flagellation woodcut. The same motifs migrate across the oeuvre: the turbaned man also appears in the Baptism (W2), the equestrian soldier in armour reappears in the Stoning (W9), and the fire-stoking youths reappear with alterations in Apollo and Coronis (W16) and the Munich Flight into Egypt (W20). Seventeen copies or versions are listed in the catalogue entry. The fullest-documented picture in this study, and the hinge of the early Rome years.

## W7 -- Judith Slaying Holofernes, c. 1601-3

Oil on copper, 24 x 18 cm (REPORTED-BUT-UNVERIFIED -- wahooart / wikioo / topimpressionists listings agree; no museum page read end to end). Wellington Museum (Apsley House), London. Judith beheading the sleeping Holofernes in his tent, the body thrust toward the viewer. Rubens owned this picture, and it shaped his own Judith and, through him, Johann Liss's Judith in the Tent of Holofernes (National Gallery, NG 4597). The violence is staged the Elsheimer way: not as a grand gesture but as a crowded, lamplit interior on a copper plate a handspan wide, the drama concentrated rather than enlarged.

## W8 -- Tobias and the Angel (the "small" Tobias), 1602-03 or 1607-08

Oil on copper, 12.1 x 19 cm (REPORTED-BUT-UNVERIFIED -- Burlington Magazine 2021 article, via search result; the Historisches Museum Frankfurt object page was not opened end to end). Historisches Museum, Frankfurt am Main. Date FLAG -- the sources split: Wikipedia, visual-arts-cork and niceartgallery date the small Tobias 1602-1603; the Burlington Magazine article of June 2021 (Garcia-Torano, Pascual Chenel and Rodriguez Rebollo, via adam-elsheimer.de) dates it 1607-08, and the Agnes Etherington Art Centre dates Goudt's engraving after it to 1608. The 1607-08 dating is the more recent scholarly position; the 1602-03 dating is the older received one. Do not quote a single date without the flag. Tobias and the angel Raphael crossing the river, the fish hanging from Tobias's staff (Tobit chapter 6, on the way to Ecbatana). Wikipedia calls this the picture "especially well received because of its new conception of landscape", and the Burlington article confirms it was engraved by Goudt and "published across Europe". The landscape is the subject as much as the figures: a lush, wooded river valley at dusk, the two small travellers threading through it. The Agnes notes that Goudt's print after it "has endured as the single most significant work of art depicting a scene from Tobit". A lost fourth treatment of the subject is proposed in the same Burlington article, known from a drawing in the Biblioteca Nacional de Espana and a mezzotint of c. 1685; the earliest Tobias version was one of a ten-panel furniture series of 1605 (see B7).

## W9 -- The Stoning of Saint Stephen, c. 1603-04

Oil on silvered copper, 34.7 x 28.6 cm (REPORTED-BUT-UNVERIFIED -- Docslib lecture summary (David R. Marshall, National Galleries of Scotland, 2015) and the academia.edu "Landscape in Rome" paper, both via search results; the NGS object page read end to end (53 lines) gives no dimensions in its extracted text). National Gallery of Scotland, Edinburgh (inv. no. 2281). Stephen kneels, already wounded and falling forward, eyes raised to a vision of the open heavens, while a beam of intense light shines on him and angels tumble toward him with palm fronds and a laurel crown. Around him a crowd of onlookers -- hostile, bemused, indifferent -- fills the middle ground; youths clamber into trees for a better view; at far right an athletic figure stretches on his toes, poised to bring a huge stone crashing down on Stephen's skull, its trajectory countered by the opposing diagonal of the shaft of heavenly light. The NGS text calls it stage-management: a shadowy Saul at left foreground, a turbaned figure on horseback, the crowd pulling the eye deep into the picture. The background of ruins is reminiscent of Paul Bril, and has been attributed to Bril himself. Rembrandt's first dated work, his own Stoning of St Stephen of 1625, is a response to this picture; Jacob Pynas's 1617 version copies its arrangement. The largest of the Edinburgh pictures and the first of the great crowd-in-landscape compositions.

## W10 -- The Burning of Troy, c. 1604

Oil on copper, 36 x 50 cm (REPORTED-BUT-UNVERIFIED -- Wikipedia caption; no Pinakothek page read end to end). Alte Pinakothek, Munich. Date FLAG: c. 1604 (Wikipedia caption) vs 1601 (visual-arts-cork list). Troy in flames at night, Aeneas carrying his father Anchises out of the burning city (the wahooart/artsdot listings title it "Aeneas Saving Anchises from Burning Troy", apparently the same picture under a variant title). At 36 x 50 cm it is the largest single-panel painting in the oeuvre apart from the Exaltation central panel. The subject is fire as light: the whole city becomes a light source, the figures small against the conflagration. The Foister catalogue notes a Landsknecht figure in the Saint Lawrence dressed like the German soldiers of the early sixteenth century, "another example of such a figure is the Landsknecht in Elsheimer's Burning of Troy at Munich" -- the picture is also a costume piece, a German soldier set down in a classical catastrophe.

## W11 -- The Denial of Peter, 1605

Oil on copper (per the oeuvre-wide pattern; no source read in this leg states the medium for this picture specifically). Dimensions NOT VERIFIED -- no dimension figure was found in the material read. Stadelsches Kunstinstitut, Frankfurt am Main. Peter denying Christ, a night scene of the kind Elsheimer was making his own by 1605. The Wikipedia examples list is the only source read that names it with a date and a location; no description of the composition was found in the material read, and it is recorded here on that thin basis rather than invented. Do not describe its composition beyond the subject until a better source is read.

## W12 -- The Flight into Egypt (daytime, oval), c. 1605

Oil on silvered copper, oval. Only 9.8 cm high (Wikipedia); wikioo gives 9 x 7 cm; Judith Dobrzynski gives an oval "measuring 3 inches by 3.88 inches" (7.6 x 9.9 cm) (REPORTED-BUT-UNVERIFIED -- the three figures disagree slightly; the Kimbell object page read end to end (15 lines) gives no dimensions in its extracted text). Kimbell Art Museum, Fort Worth, Texas (purchased 1994). Date FLAG: c. 1605 (Wikipedia) vs 1605-1608 (ASP paper). The daytime Flight, and the earlier of the two attempts at the subject: the Holy Family taking an arduous path through rocky terrain in full sun, broad-brimmed pilgrim hats warding off the glare, carpenter's tools, water gourd and cup, rustic harness and saddle marking their poverty, a mighty oak singled out by divine light -- the Tree of Life, from whose wood the cross was made (Kimbell text). The ASP paper calls it "Elsheimer's second approach to the subject" the other way round -- the daytime version first, the Munich nocturne second -- which is the correct order: this is the first Flight, the Munich picture the rethinking. One of only two Elsheimer paintings on public display outside Europe (the other is the Mocking of Ceres in Kingston).

## W13 -- The Exaltation of the Cross (Frankfurt Tabernacle / Altarpiece of the Holy Cross), c. 1605

Oil on copper (seven panels: central panel plus six surrounding scenes). Central panel 48 x 35 cm (REPORTED-BUT-UNVERIFIED -- Wikipedia caption). FLAG: the artsdot/wahooart listings give 133 x 107 cm for "The Altarpiece of the Exaltation of the True Cross" -- that is presumably the whole assembled altarpiece, from a low-reliability source; do not quote it as the panel size. Stadel Museum, Frankfurt am Main (reunited 1950-1981; two panels were in the Stadel from the start, five were bought back between 1971 and 1981). Elsheimer's greatest work by the Stadel's own account, and the exception that proves the smallness rule: a domestic altarpiece of seven copper panels, documented but long presumed lost, reunited by the Stadel between 1950 and 1981, its modern frame reconstructed after sketches made in 1612. The central panel shows the exaltation of the Cross; the six scenes around it recount the legend of the Finding of the Cross by St Helena and its later return to Jerusalem by Emperor Heraclius (individual panels: The Embarkation of the Empress Helena, The Digging for the Cross, Heraclius on Horseback with the Cross, Emperor Heraclius' Entry into Jerusalem). The Stadel text: "With their striking compositions and dramatic lighting, the panels represent special highlights of Elsheimer's late period." The Finding of the Cross is therefore not a separate painting but the subject of this altarpiece's side panels.

## W14 -- The Mocking of Ceres, c. 1605-08 (two versions)

Oil on copper coated with a tin-lead alloy (silvered copper) -- Agnes version; oil on copperplate -- Prado version. Agnes version 29.1 x 24 cm (VERIFIED -- Agnes Etherington object page read end to end, 59 lines). Prado version 30 x 25 cm (VERIFIED -- Prado object page read end to end, 139 lines). Original: Agnes Etherington Art Centre, Kingston, Ontario (acc. 51-004.03; gift of Alfred and Isabel Bader, 2008). Workshop version: Museo Nacional del Prado, Madrid (P002181, "Adam Elsheimer and workshop", Room 078). Ceres, searching for her abducted daughter Proserpina, stops at a doorway and drinks greedily from a jug of water offered by an old woman holding a candle; beside her the naked youth Stellio points and laughs at her thirst, and Ceres turns him into a lizard (Ovid, Metamorphoses V: 425-486). The Agnes text stresses the tenebrism: candlelight and torchlight against darkness, "the intense contrast of light and shadow popularized by Caravaggio in Rome in the early 1590s", with a woman milking a cow in the background and a burning torch resting on wooden wheels. The picture was part of the Dutch Gift to Charles II in 1660 and was badly damaged by fire at some point in its history. The Prado version belonged to Rubens. Klessmann's 1997 Stadel-Jahrbuch article ("Elsheimer: die Verspottung der Ceres -- zur Frage des Originals") settled the original/copy question in favour of Kingston; Wikipedia's older text still describes the Kingston picture as in a US private collection with a copy in the Prado, which the 2008 Bader gift superseded. Goudt engraved the composition in 1610, with Elsheimer's knowledge, while he still lived; the print follows the painting precisely.

## W15 -- Aurora, 1606

Oil on copper, 170 x 225 mm (17 x 22.5 cm) (REPORTED-BUT-UNVERIFIED -- academia.edu "Landscape in Rome: Adam Elsheimer and Paul Bril" snippet, via search result; the Herzog Anton Ulrich-Museum object page was not opened end to end). Herzog Anton Ulrich-Museum, Brunswick (Braunschweig). Aurora, goddess of dawn, leading the chariot of the sun -- the dawn piece to set against the nocturnes. The academia.edu paper cites it alongside the Stoning and Il Contento as a picture in which ruins begin to appear in the backgrounds after the Rome arrival. Little descriptive material was found in the sources read; it is recorded on the strength of the date, location, medium and size above. Its importance for evolution is the subject itself: Elsheimer painting the dawn as a companion to the night, light arriving rather than light departing.

## W16 -- Apollo and Coronis, 1606-08

Oil on copper, 12 x 17 cm (REPORTED-BUT-UNVERIFIED -- allpaintingsstore / artsdot listings; one topimpressionists listing gives 179 x 230 cm, which is plainly wrong for a cabinet copper and is disregarded). Walker Art Gallery, Liverpool. Date FLAG: 1606-08 (Wikipedia) vs 1607 (wahooart, artsdot, visual-arts-cork) vs 1608 (topimpressionists); the range 1606-08 covers the spread. Apollo discovering the pregnant Coronis's infidelity and striking her down (Ovid) -- the instant of the killing, set in a landscape bordering a tranquil river or lake, the composition deliberately asymmetrical, diagonals carrying the unease (wahooart subject essay). The Foister catalogue notes that the youths stoking the fire in the Saint Lawrence "appear again with alterations in Apollo and Coronis" -- Elsheimer reuses his own figure stock across pictures. The visual-arts-cork survey singles it out: "Elsheimer's treatment of enlarging figures on the landscape, as in Apollo and Coronis (1607-08), anticipates Van Dyck and Rubens" -- the figures here are bigger against the landscape than in the early pictures, the direction that leads, through Rubens, to the English portrait tradition (Wikipedia, Influence section).

## W17 -- Latona and the Lycian Peasants, 1607/1608

Oil on copper (per the oeuvre pattern; the Met's Hollar etching record describes it as "a painting on copper by Elsheimer in the Wallraf-Richartz Museum, Cologne"). Dimensions NOT VERIFIED -- no dimension figure for the painting was found in the material read. (Hollar's 1649 etching after it measures 17.1 x 23.2 cm sheet; that is the print, not the painting.) Wallraf-Richartz Museum, Cologne. Latona, with her children Apollo and Diana, kneeling by a river and raising both arms as the Lycian peasants who denied her water are turned into frogs in the marshes (Ovid, Metamorphoses VI). The British Museum's description of a related print gives the clearest picture of the composition: Latona on an island at left, the peasants half-man half-frog on the river bank, the marsh landscape filling the frame. Wenceslaus Hollar etched it in 1649, and the Fitzwilliam notes a related painting at the Courtauld currently attributed to Johann Konig -- a second-version question. Another Ovid subject never painted before Elsheimer, in the same line as Philemon and Baucis and Ceres.

## W18 -- Jupiter and Mercury in the House of Philemon and Baucis, c. 1608

Oil on copper, 17 x 22 cm (Wikipedia) vs 16 x 22 cm (wahooart / allpaintingsstore) (REPORTED-BUT-UNVERIFIED -- the two figures differ by a centimetre in height; no Dresden object page read end to end). Gemaldegalerie Alte Meister, Dresden (Staatliche Kunstsammlungen, inv. no. 1977). Date FLAG: c. 1608 (Wikipedia; NGA Rembrandt entry) vs 1609 (wahooart / wikioo listings); 1608 is the scholarly dating, 1609 the listing dating. The gods Jupiter and Mercury, turned away by richer households, welcomed by the old couple Philemon and Baucis in their humble cottage (Ovid, Metamorphoses VIII). Elsheimer shows the earlier moment, before the couple know their guests are gods: the gods lounge in the corner of the dimly lit interior while Baucis offers a blanket and Philemon is outside in the dark with a candle, gathering food (Te Papa description of Goudt's 1612 engraving after it). Wikipedia: the episode "had never been painted before". Goudt engraved it in 1612; the painting was in Goudt's possession by 1612, one of several he took from Rome to the Netherlands. Rembrandt knew it through Goudt's engraving and made at least three drawings after it, and his own Philemon and Baucis of 1658 takes its composition from Elsheimer, switching the positions of Jupiter and Mercury (NGA). The interior here is the counterpart to the nocturnal landscapes: the same dim, multi-source lamplight, but indoors, with the gods as guests rather than the heavens as backdrop.

## W19 -- Il Contento (Contentment), c. 1605-10

Oil on copper, 30.00 x 42.00 cm (VERIFIED -- NGS object page read end to end, 44 lines). National Gallery of Scotland, Edinburgh (NG 2312; accepted in lieu of inheritance tax, 1970). Date FLAG: NGS "About 1605-10"; wahooart 1607; NGS spotlight: begun c. 1605, unfinished at death 1610. The picture was never finished. An episode from Mateo Aleman's picaresque novel Guzman de Alfarache (Madrid 1599; Italian version 1606): the people of Earth worship the god Contento more than Jupiter, so jealous Jupiter sends Mercury to abduct Contento and replace him with his twin Discontento. Elsheimer was the first artist ever to depict the story, and he turned Contento into a female goddess. Jupiter hovers in mid-air at left, directing Mercury -- winged hat -- to pull Contento away from the devoted crowd, some clinging to her cloak in despair; in the background people feast and play sports and games, unaware of their imminent discontentment (the NGS spotlight says Elsheimer took the Roman carnival for inspiration). A preparatory drawing shows he first devised a less dynamic composition, two groups moving toward the centre from opposite directions. The picture is the largest of the Edinburgh pair with the Stoning, and the most ambitious: a crowd scene, a hovering god, a kidnapping in progress, and a feast going on behind it -- all on a 30 x 42 cm copper, left unfinished at his death and recorded in that state by Johann Konig in a drawing of 1610.

## W20 -- The Flight into Egypt (Munich nocturne), c. 1609

Oil on copper, 31 x 41 cm (Wikipedia article; MFAH Bulletin, Fall 1974; Scribner, Rubens) (REPORTED-BUT-UNVERIFIED -- three sources agree on 31 x 41 cm, but dailyartmagazine gives 30.6 x 41.5 cm and Dobrzynski 12 1/4 x 16 1/4 in (31.1 x 41.3 cm); no Alte Pinakothek object page read end to end). Alte Pinakothek, Munich (Bayerische Staatsgemaldesammlungen). The masterpiece and probably the last painting. The Holy Family by night: Joseph holds a torch that lights Mary and the infant riding an ass at centre; a campfire burns near shepherds at left, toward which the family heads; the moon, accurately depicted with its mottled surface, reflects off calm water; and above it all the Milky Way runs diagonally across the sky, echoing the diagonal of the almost-black treed landscape that contains the foreground figures. Four sources of light -- moon, fire, torch, water reflection -- and constellations rendered accurately enough to date: Ursa Major at far left, Leo above the Holy Family with Regulus at the centre of the picture. It is thought to be the first naturalistic rendering of the night sky in Renaissance art, and the first accurate depiction of constellations; the 2005 Alte Pinakothek / Deutsches Museum investigation concluded Elsheimer must have had access to a telescope, since the sky matches the night over Rome in June 1609. The rework hypothesis -- that he repainted it in 1610 after Galileo's Sidereus Nuncius showed the Milky Way as individual stars -- is contested by Elsheimer scholar Keith Andrews, and Howard and Longair 2011 argue the sky could never have been observed as painted. At his death the picture hung in his bedroom; his widow asked 300 scudi for it (Rubens to Faber, 14 January 1611); it passed to Goudt, who took it to Utrecht and engraved it in 1613 -- the engraving that taught Aert van der Neer and a whole Dutch school to paint the night, and the doorway of this study. Rembrandt's Flight into Egypt of 1627, Claude Lorrain, Rubens, and (much later) Caspar David Friedrich's Evening on the Baltic Sea all descend from it.

## Beyond the 20

- **B1 -- Tobias and the Angel, the "Large Tobias" (lost original).** Known only through Goudt's engraving of 1613 and two copies, one in the National Gallery, London (NG 1424, oil on copper, 19.3 x 27.6 cm, second half of the 17th century) and one in the Statens Museum for Kunst, Copenhagen. The Morgan's Goudt drawing was the preparatory study for the 1613 engraving "after the lost painting... known as the Large Tobias". The NG copy text notes the large tree at right has been identified as a eucalyptus -- Australian, unknown in Europe then -- possibly through Elsheimer's friend the botanist Johann Faber. Not counted in the 20 because the original does not survive.
- **B2 -- Venus and Cupid, c. 1600, Berlin.** Listed in the Wikipedia examples ("Venus and Cupid, c. 1600, Berlin") and in the hellenica/augusta painting lists. No dimensions and no description of the composition were found in the material read; recorded here as named-only.
- **B3 -- Self-Portrait, c. 1606-07, Galleria degli Uffizi, Florence.** His only portrait and his only painting on canvas (Wikipedia); given to the Accademia di San Luca on his admission in 1606. Not counted in the 20 because it is a portrait on canvas, outside the copper cabinet series this leg documents, and no dimensions were found in the material read.
- **B4 -- Landscape with the Temple of Vesta in Tivoli, c. 1600.** Named in the hellenica/augusta lists and dated c. 1600 by artble. The version in the National Gallery, Prague (O 195) is catalogued as "painting by the circle of Adam Elsheimer", not autograph (Wikidata, via search result). Not counted in the 20 because the surviving version is circle, not autograph.
- **B5 -- Landscape with the Flight into Egypt.** Named in the hellenica/augusta painting lists; no date, location or dimensions established in the material read. Possibly a variant title for one of the two Flight pictures (W12, W20); do not treat it as a third Flight without further evidence.
- **B6 -- Glorification of the Cross, c. 1605.** Named in the niceartgallery list ("Glorification of the Cross c. 1605") with no location. Possibly a variant title for the Exaltation central panel (W13); do not treat it as a separate work without further evidence.
- **B7 -- Set of small religious scenes, c. 1605, Petworth House.** Wikipedia examples list ("Set of small religious scenes, 1605, Petworth House"). The Burlington Magazine 2021 article says the earliest Tobias version formed part of a series of Old and New Testament scenes, originally ten panels, probably painted in 1605 to decorate the front of a piece of furniture, of which only some survive. Counted as a group, not as a single work, and the individual panel subjects were not established in the material read.
- **B8 -- Ceres Changes Stellio into a Lizard, 1605/1608, National Gallery of Art, Washington.** A drawing, not a painting: gouache and pen and brown ink on brown prepared paper, sheet 11 x 6.8 cm, Wolfgang Ratjen Collection, acc. 2007.111.9 (NGA artist page and Google Arts and Culture record, via search results). Recorded here because it is the NGA's only Elsheimer and a study for the Ceres pictures, but not counted among the 20 paintings.
- **B9 -- Landscape with the Good Samaritan.** Wikidata lists it as a notable work of Elsheimer, and the Rijksmuseum holds a version (SK-A-2650) -- but the Rijksmuseum catalogues that picture as "by a follower of Adam Elsheimer", not autograph. Not counted in the 20: the autograph Good Samaritan, if it exists, was not located in the material read. FLAG: the Good Samaritan could not be confirmed as an autograph Elsheimer painting.
- **B10 -- Landscape with bathing nymph / Nymph Fleeing Satyrs.** Named in the augusta list ("Landscape with bathing nymph") and the visual-arts-cork list ("Nymph Fleeing Satyrs (State Museum, Berlin)"). No date, dimensions or description found in the material read; recorded as named-only.

Further titles named in passing in sources: Apollo and the Cattle of Admetus (hellenica paintings list; nothing further established); Preaching of John the Baptist (augusta paintings list; nothing further established); House on the mountain, Tondo (augusta paintings list; nothing further established); Saint Lawrence on the gridiron, c. 1599 -- a distinct early version after Titian's Venetian altarpiece, Venice period, cited by Seifert in the Foister 2024 Saint Lawrence entry; not the same picture as W6; The Embarkation of the Empress Helena; The Digging for the Cross; Heraclius on Horseback with the Cross; Emperor Heraclius' Entry into Jerusalem -- the four named side panels of the Frankfurt Tabernacle (W13), Stadel digital collection; Tobias and the Angel, gouache preparatory work, Staatliche Museen, Berlin -- cited by the Agnes Etherington Art Centre as the likely preparatory work for the small Tobias (W8); Aeneas Saving Anchises from Burning Troy -- the wahooart/artsdot title for what is apparently the Burning of Troy (W10) under a variant title; do not count as a separate picture; Holy Family with Angels -- named in the hellenica/augusta lists; probably a variant title for W3; do not count as a separate picture; Judith and Holofernes (Wikidata "inferred from" entry) -- the same picture as W7 under a variant title; Ceres and Stellio -- the wikioo/wahooart title for the Prado Ceres picture (W14, workshop version); do not count as a separate picture.

## Evolution

**The format never changes; everything else does.** From the first dated picture (W1, Rest on the Flight, 1599) to the last (W20, Munich Flight, c. 1609) Elsheimer paints small oils on copper, almost all of them between 12 and 50 cm on the long side. The only exceptions in the material read are the Uffizi Self-Portrait, his only painting on canvas (see B3), and the seven-panel Frankfurt Tabernacle (W13), which is seven small coppers assembled, not one big picture. The largest single panel in the 20 is the Burning of Troy at 36 x 50 cm; the smallest is the Kimbell Flight at under 10 cm high. Scale is not a phase; it is the condition of the whole career. What changes is what he puts on the copper.

**Early (Frankfurt / Venice, to c. 1600): Venetian colour, devotional subjects, landscape as setting.** The earliest small paintings on copper seem to date from before he arrived in Italy (Wikipedia), and the two Venice pictures, the Baptism (W2) and the Holy Family (W3), show Tintoretto, Veronese and Rottenhammer: warm colour, soft modelling, figures arranged close to the plane. The subjects are conventional -- Baptism, Holy Family, Rest on the Flight, St Paul on Malta -- and the landscape, though already prominent, is a setting for the figures rather than the argument of the picture. A second early St Lawrence, of the saint on the gridiron closely following Titian's Venetian altarpiece, dated c. 1599 by Seifert, belongs to this Venetian moment (Foister 2024).

**Rome, first years (1600-1604): ruins arrive, the crowd arrives, the revisions show.** In early 1600 Elsheimer arrives in Rome. The St Lawrence (W6, c. 1601-2) is the evidence of the transition in the paint itself: the catalogue's infrared reflectography and photomicrographs show the angel's arm moved, the emperor moved down, a canopy painted late over a continuous landscape, the red cloak added over the gridiron, the turbaned man's leg reposed three times. He is working the composition out on the copper, and the Roman references -- the Hercules statue, the temple of Vespasian, the SPQR banner, the tombs of the via Appia -- are being learned as he paints. The academia.edu "Landscape in Rome" paper dates the change precisely: "Not too long after Elsheimer's arrival in Rome, ruins began to appear in the backgrounds of his paintings, for example the Stoning of St. Stephen and Il Contento both in Edinburgh, and the True Cross panels, now in Frankfurt." The Stoning (W9, c. 1603-04) is the first great crowd picture: the saint small and kneeling, the crowd filling the middle ground, youths in trees, the light doing the moral work. Its background has been attributed to Paul Bril -- collaboration, or at least the direct influence of the older Flemish landscapist in Rome. The small Tobias (W8) is praised in its own time for a "new conception of landscape": the figures are travellers in a landscape that has its own weather and depth, not actors on a stage.

**Middle (1604-1607): fire, night, and subjects nobody had painted.** The Burning of Troy (W10, c. 1604) makes a whole city into a light source. The Denial of Peter (W11, 1605) and the Petworth furniture panels (1605) extend the small religious scene. The Frankfurt Tabernacle (W13, c. 1605) is the most ambitious undertaking of the career -- seven panels, a domestic altarpiece, the Finding of the Cross told in six scenes around a central Exaltation -- and the Stadel calls its panels "special highlights of Elsheimer's late period", which already blurs any neat early/late line: the biggest work sits in the middle of the decade. The Ceres pictures (W14, c. 1605-08) bring Caravaggesque tenebrism into the cabinet: candlelight and torchlight, a nocturnal doorway, the goddess drinking greedily. Aurora (W15, 1606) paints the dawn as the companion of the night. And the subject-matter turns, decisively, to the unpainted: Wikipedia's summary is exact -- "Elsheimer had a definite preference for choosing rare or original subjects" -- Philemon and Baucis "had never been painted before", and the Mocking of Ceres, Apollo and Coronis and Il Contento "were equally new".

**Late (1607-1610): figures grow, light multiplies, the landscape swallows the story.** Apollo and Coronis (W16, 1606-08) enlarges the figures against the landscape, the move the visual-arts-cork survey says "anticipates Van Dyck and Rubens". Latona (W17, 1607/08) and Jupiter and Mercury (W18, c. 1608) push the Ovid line further, the second of them indoors, the gods lounging in a corner of a dim cottage while the old couple work in the dark -- the nocturne turned inward. Il Contento (W19), begun c. 1605 and still unfinished at his death, is the ambition outrunning the method: a crowd, a hovering god, an abduction, a carnival feast, on 30 x 42 cm of copper, reworked from a calmer first design to a more dynamic one and never completed. And then the Munich Flight (W20, c. 1609): the figures are tiny, the Holy Family occupying the lower third, the landscape and the sky doing everything else -- four light sources, the Milky Way as a diagonal band, constellations accurate to the night over Rome in June 1609. The arc of the decade is from figures in a landscape, through figures and landscape in balance, to figures lost inside a landscape and a sky that is itself the subject. The nocturne is not a late discovery -- Jacob's Dream (W5, c. 1600) is already a night scene -- but it is a late mastery: the early nights are lit by one fire or one beam, the last night by moon, fire, torch and still water at once, with the stars placed where the telescope would find them.

**The figure stock is reused, not reinvented.** The Foister catalogue's cross-references are the clearest evidence of a working method: the turbaned man of the Baptism reappears in the Saint Lawrence; the equestrian soldier of the Saint Lawrence reappears in the Stoning; the fire-stoking youths of the Saint Lawrence reappear in Apollo and Coronis and the Munich Flight. Elsheimer builds a small company of figures and moves them from picture to picture, changing the light and the landscape around them. The figures themselves stay short, stocky and unflamboyant throughout (Wikipedia) -- no classical idealization creeps in late. What grows is the landscape's share of the picture and the number of lights in it.

## Disputes and unverified items (flagged)

- **F1 -- Oeuvre size.** "About forty paintings are now generally agreed to be by him" (Wikipedia, citing Klessmann); "Few more than thirty finished paintings on copper... have survived" (Kimbell); "only 30 paintings" (Daily Art Magazine); "only twenty-seven pictures are attributed to him" (mydailyartdisplay, search snippet); "just 30 to 40 works" (Dobrzynski/WSJ, search snippet). No single count is settled.
- **F2 -- Flight (Munich) dimensions.** 31 cm x 41 cm (Wikipedia; Howard and Longair 2011; MFAH bulletin 12 3/16 x 16 1/8 in) vs 30.6 cm x 41.5 cm (Daily Art Magazine, search snippet). Minor, but the sources differ.
- **F3 -- Flight (Munich) date.** c. 1609, inscribed on the back "Adam Elsheimer fecit Romae 1609" (Howard and Longair 2011) vs "1609-10" (MFAH bulletin, search snippet). The reworking question (P3) is part of this.
- **F4 -- Kimbell Flight into Egypt.** "Oil on silvered copper, c. 1605, only 9.8 cm high" (Wikipedia examples list) vs "dated 1605-1608" and a daytime scene (Howard and Longair 2011, ASP paper). Dobrzynski (search snippet) calls it "an oval measuring 3 inches by 3.88 inches, that was purchased in 1994".
- **F5 -- Debtor's prison.** Wikipedia says the borrowing from Goudt "according to one account resulted in his brief incarceration in Debtor's prison." No other source read confirms imprisonment; Kimbell and others say only that he died in poverty / difficult circumstances. Treat imprisonment as single-account, unconfirmed.
- **F6 -- Telescope.** Dobrzynski (search snippet) reports that in 2005 the Alte Pinakothek and the Deutsches Museum "combined to prove conclusively that he must have had access to a telescope" and that the sky is Rome on 16 June 1609. Howard and Longair (2011) argue the opposite: the sky is not observable as painted and the Milky Way was painted before the trees, so no 1610 telescopic reworking. These positions conflict directly.
- **F7 -- Conversion to Catholicism.** "By 1608 (possibly 1606)" (Wikipedia). Unresolved between the two years.
- **F8 -- Wife's name.** "Carola Antonia Stuarda da Francoforte" (Wikipedia body) vs "Carla Antonia Stuart" (Wikipedia infobox). Same person, spelling differs.
- **F9 -- The son born 1609.** Not mentioned in a census a year later; Klessmann's wet-nurse explanation is flagged in Wikipedia itself as "optimistic". The child's fate is unknown.
- **F10 -- Venice stay.** "Undocumented" (Wikipedia) but "for about two years" (NG); "probably worked as an assistant to Johann Rottenhammer" (Wikipedia). The duration and the assistantship are both probable, not documented.
- **F11 -- Goudt's role and dates.** "Elsheimer's pupil" (NGI); "may have been the German painter's student but was also an avid patron" (Met); "lodged and trained with him for several years" (Wikipedia). Goudt's dates are given as 1583-1648 (Met) vs 1585-1648 (NGA). Both the role and the birth year differ between museum sources.
- **F12 -- Hans Fugger as patron.** NO evidence found. Hans Fugger (1531-1598) died the year Elsheimer left for Italy; the Fugger patronage documented in the sources read concerns earlier artists (Paolo Fiammingo's Planets for Hans Fugger, 1591-92). The documented patron of Elsheimer is Cardinal Francesco Maria del Monte (Wikipedia infobox; del Monte article citing Howard 1992). Do not list Fugger as a patron.
- **F13 -- "First" claims, three different ones.** "First naturalistic rendering of the night sky in Renaissance art" (Wikipedia/Howard); "first true moonlit scene" / "first realistic moonlit scene in European painting" (McCouat; Dobrzynski); "first painter to accurately depict constellations" (Wikipedia). These are distinct claims from different sources; the Milky Way claim is separately contested (F6).
- **F14 -- Rubens and etching.** A 1907 Rubens biography (archive.org, search snippet only) says "Elsheimer... was a practiced etcher, and Rubens himself testifies that he initiated him into the technical mysteries of that craft." This is snippet-only, unverified, and sits oddly against Wikipedia's "He made a few etchings, but not very successfully." Do not use without verification.
- **F15 -- Rembrandt's Stoning of St Stephen.** Wikipedia says it "appears to be a response to Elsheimer's painting of the subject" -- hedged, not certain.
- **F16 -- Wikipedia infobox lists "Education: Friedrich Brentel, Johann Rottenhammer", but the article body says he was apprenticed to Philipp Uffenbach and only "probably" assisted Rottenhammer; Brentel is not mentioned in the body at all. Infobox and body disagree.
- **F17 -- "Small" Tobias and the Angel.** Dated 1602-1603 in the Wikipedia body ("especially well received because of its new conception of landscape") but listed as "1607/8, Frankfurt" in the same article's examples list. The two dates in one source conflict.
- **F18 -- Goudt engraving date.** The Flight engraving is dated 1613 (Met; NGI), but the Met's "More Artwork" listing also shows Goudt after Elsheimer works dated 1612, and some sources give the seven engravings as 1612-1613 collectively. The Flight itself is 1613 in both museum records read.
- **F19 -- Small Tobias date (leg 2).** 1602-03 (Wikipedia, visual-arts-cork, niceartgallery) vs 1607-08 (Burlington Magazine, June 2021; Agnes: Goudt's engraving dated 1608). The recent scholarly position is 1607-08; the older received date is 1602-03. Same dispute as F17, recorded from the works leg.
- **F20 -- Saint Lawrence date.** Andrews c. 1600; Drost c. 1602-3; Weiszacker Venice period; Levey c. 1600; Klessmann close to the Stoning (1603-4); Foister 2024 c. 1601-2. The spread is the whole first half of the Rome decade.
- **F21 -- Three Marys at the Tomb is NOT an Elsheimer.** The title belongs to the Van Eycks (Museum Boijmans Van Beuningen, Rotterdam, attributed to Jan van Eyck, 1425-35). No Elsheimer picture of that subject was found in any source read. One church bulletin (First Parish, St Augustine, 1 April 2018) carries a cover caption reading "Adam Elsheimer (German, c. 1603), oil on copper" next to the Three Marys title -- a misplaced caption, not evidence of an Elsheimer picture.
- **F22 -- Prodigal Son and David and Goliath.** No Elsheimer painting of either subject was confirmed in any source read this run, despite two targeted searches. Do not include either as a documented Elsheimer work. (The "Finding of the Cross" is covered: it is the subject of the Frankfurt Tabernacle side panels, W13.)
- **F23 -- Stoning of St Stephen background.** The academia.edu "Landscape in Rome" paper says the background "has been attributed to Bril" -- a collaboration or attribution question, not settled in the material read.
- **F24 -- Burning of Troy date.** c. 1604 (Wikipedia caption) vs 1601 (visual-arts-cork list). The 1604 dating is the one carried by the museum caption tradition.
- **F25 -- Latona second version.** The Fitzwilliam notes a painting related to the Cologne Latona at the Courtauld Institute, London, "currently attributed to Johan Konig" -- a copy or second version, attribution unresolved in the material read.
- **F26 -- Goudt's dates vary by source.** 1583-1648 (Te Papa, British Museum) vs 1585-1630 (Detroit Institute of Arts, University of Michigan Museum of Art). The 1630 death year cannot stand -- Goudt was alive and working in the 1640s; the 1648 death year is the one carried by the Met and Wikipedia.
- **F27 -- Holy Family, Berlin, dimensions and date.** 37.5 x 24 cm (Wikipedia caption) vs 37.8 x 24.4 cm (Wikidata); date 1599 vs 1600 (two Wikidata references).
- **F28 -- Exaltation / Altarpiece size.** Central panel 48 x 35 cm (Wikipedia caption) vs whole altarpiece 133 x 107 cm (artsdot, low reliability). The Stadel's seven-panel structure is the reliable fact; the 133 x 107 cm figure should not be quoted without its source.
- **F29 -- St Paul on Malta medium.** allpaintingsstore and wahooart say "acrylic on canvas", which is wrong for a c. 1600 picture; the correct medium is oil on copper (Pollock book list, National Gallery catalogue tradition).
- **F30 -- Apollo and Coronis size.** One topimpressionists listing gives 179 x 230 cm, which is impossible for a cabinet copper and is disregarded -- the consistent figure is 12 x 17 cm.
- **F31 -- Jupiter and Mercury date and size.** c. 1608 (Wikipedia, NGA) vs 1609 (wahooart, wikioo); 17 x 22 cm (Wikipedia) vs 16 x 22 cm (wahooart, allpaintingsstore).
- **F32 -- Il Contento date.** NGS "About 1605-10"; wahooart 1607; NGS spotlight: begun c. 1605, unfinished at death 1610.
- **F33 -- Mocking of Ceres, original vs copy.** The Agnes (Kingston) picture is the original per Klessmann 1997; the Prado picture is "Adam Elsheimer and workshop". Wikipedia's older text still describes the Kingston picture as in a US private collection with a copy in the Prado; the 2008 Bader gift to Agnes superseded that. The Kingston picture was badly damaged by fire at some point in its history (Wikipedia).
- **F34 -- Venus and Cupid and the Good Samaritan.** Both appear in lists as Elsheimer works, but neither could be documented with a date, dimensions and description in the material read; the Rijksmuseum Good Samaritan is by a follower. Both are in "Beyond the 20", not in the 20.
- **F35 -- Goudt engravings count (leg 3).** Seven (Elsheimer Wikipedia, Met) vs a series of five prints (Goudt Wikipedia). Unresolved; it may be seven total against five in one series, but no source read establishes that reconciliation.
- **F36 -- Brentel as Elsheimer's teacher rests on the Elsheimer Wikipedia infobox only.** The article body never mentions Brentel, and the 1596 Strasbourg visit is itself only "probably". Thin link; Brentel is reserve, not main, for #553.
- **F37 -- Del Monte as Elsheimer's patron rests on the Elsheimer Wikipedia infobox only.** No narrative evidence was found in this run.
- **F38 -- Rottenhammer article title.** The Wikipedia article is "Hans Rottenhammer", not "Johann Rottenhammer"; a browser.open for the Johann title failed (page not found). The Rottenhammer facts in this study rest on search snippets, not an end-to-end page read.

## Dimensions verified vs not verified (tally)

- **VERIFIED (3 of 20):** W6 Saint Lawrence prepared for Martyrdom (26.8 x 20.9 cm, National Gallery catalogue read end to end); W14 Mocking of Ceres, Agnes version (29.1 x 24 cm, Agnes page read end to end) with the Prado workshop version also verified (30 x 25 cm, Prado page read end to end); W19 Il Contento (30 x 42 cm, NGS page read end to end).
- **REPORTED-BUT-UNVERIFIED (15 of 20):** W1, W2, W3, W4, W5, W7, W8, W9, W10, W12, W13, W15, W16, W18, W20 (named source for each, in the entries above).
- **NOT VERIFIED (2 of 20):** W11 Denial of Peter, W17 Latona (no dimension figure found in the material read).
- Total: 3 + 15 + 2 = 20.

## Etchings / prints / drawings

"He made a few etchings, but not very successfully" (Wikipedia). Drawings survive in Paris (Louvre) and Edinburgh among other locations (Wikipedia). No writing by Elsheimer himself is cited in any source read; everything about his intentions comes from contemporaries (van Mander 1604, Mancini, Rubens' letters) and later scholars. The prints that matter are not his own but Goudt's: seven engravings after his paintings (1612-1613), the Flight of 1613 among them, which carried the nocturne into the Dutch print market -- see M12. A drawing, Ceres Changes Stellio into a Lizard (1605/1608, gouache and pen and brown ink on brown prepared paper, sheet 11 x 6.8 cm, Wolfgang Ratjen Collection, NGA acc. 2007.111.9), is the NGA's only Elsheimer and a study for the Ceres pictures (see B8). Wenceslaus Hollar etched the Latona in 1649. Goudt's engraving after the small Tobias is dated 1608; his engraving after Jupiter and Mercury is dated 1612; his engraving after the Mocking of Ceres is dated 1610, made with Elsheimer's knowledge while he still lived.

## Aesthetic logic

Elsheimer's logic is the logic of the small, the dark, and the counted. Everything in his practice points the same way: a copper plate no bigger than a hand, a night lit by four small lights instead of one big one, a sky made of a thousand separate stars, a story shrunk to the lower third of the frame, and a figure stock reused from picture to picture because the figures are not the point -- the light and the landscape are. The cabinet picture is not a small version of a big picture; it is a different kind of object, made to be held close, looked at slowly, and hidden in a prince's room. That privacy is the condition of the fame: because almost nobody could see the originals, the work travelled as engraving, and the engraving taught a whole school to paint the night. The nocturne is not a mood in Elsheimer; it is a system with parts -- copper ground, graded lights, counted stars, parallel diagonals, tiny figures, light from below -- and the system is what the Dutch inherited, not the pictures. The telescope dispute is the same logic from the other side: whether or not he ever looked through a lens, the sky he painted is a sky you can count, and counting is the point. The stars are placed by meaning as much as by observation -- Leo over the Holy Family, Regulus over the Christ child, the Milky Way slanting like Jacob's Ladder -- so the same sky is both a night and a sentence. The late pictures let the landscape swallow the story entirely: the figures get smaller, the lights multiply, and the sky takes the frame. That is the arc the Dutch took up -- Aert's moonlit rivers are Elsheimer's nocturne with the figures removed and the water put in -- and it is why the study of Elsheimer is the study of a transmission: a man who died at 32, in poverty, whose thirty-odd coppers taught Europe to paint the night through paper.

## Positions

- **P1 -- Deborah Howard (1992; with Malcolm Longair 2011):** the Flight is the first apparently naturalistic rendering of the night sky, and the first resolution of the Milky Way into individual stars; but the 2011 paper reverses the naive reading -- the sky could never have been observed as painted, and the picture is "more a religious iconographic text than an accurate representation of the sky."
- **P2 -- Keith Andrews (1976; 1977 monograph):** argued conclusively the sky was not observable as depicted; "what Elsheimer intended to convey was an evocation, not observable scientific fact"; contested Cavina's 1610 reworking hypothesis.
- **P3 -- Anna Ottani Cavina (1976):** proposed Elsheimer reworked the Flight in 1610 after Galileo's Sidereus Nuncius appeared, to add the resolved Milky Way and detailed Moon. Contested by Andrews; tested and doubted by Howard and Longair (2011) on the evidence of overpainting order.
- **P4 -- Rudiger Klessmann and others (2006 exhibition catalogue, the main source for the Wikipedia article):** about forty paintings are generally agreed to be by Elsheimer; the son born 1609 was "optimistically" assumed to have been put out to a wet-nurse when he vanished from the census.
- **P5 -- R. H. Wilenski (quoted in the Wikipedia Flight article):** Elsheimer's sky "is no longer a blackcloth but a symbol for boundless space."
- **P6 -- Anne Hollander (Moving Pictures, 1989/1991):** the Flight's outwardly prosaic depiction against a vast backdrop has a camera-like quality, "cinematic rather than theatrical"; its composition influenced Claude and Rubens.
- **P7 -- Giulio Mancini (contemporary, quoted on the Kimbell page):** Elsheimer's works were "in the hands of princes and those persons who, in order that they should not be taken from them, keep them hidden" -- scarcity as the condition of his fame.

## Quotes

- **Q1 -- Rubens, in a letter after Elsheimer's death (Wikipedia):** "he had no equal in small figures, landscapes, and in many other subjects. ...one could have expected things from him that one has never seen before and never will see."
- **Q2 -- Rubens lamenting Elsheimer's death (McCouat, citing Klessmann p. 38):** "I have never felt my heart more profoundly pierced by grief than at this news... Surely, after such a loss our entire profession ought to clothe itself in mourning.... He had no equal in small figures, in landscapes, and in many other subjects"
- **Q3 -- Giulio Mancini, contemporary (Kimbell page):** Elsheimer's works were "in the hands of princes and those persons who, in order that they should not be taken from them, keep them hidden."
- **Q4 -- R. H. Wilenski (Wikipedia, Flight article):** Elsheimer's sky "is no longer a blackcloth but a symbol for boundless space".
- **Q5 -- Howard and Longair (2011), abstract:** "It is argued that the sky was not one that Elsheimer could ever have seen and that the painting is more a religious iconographic text than an accurate representation of the sky."
- **Q6 -- Keith Andrews (1977), quoted in Howard and Longair (2011):** "In short, what Elsheimer intended to convey was an evocation, not observable scientific fact"
- **Q7 -- National Gallery, London, artist page:** "He combined precision of technique with inventive explorations of landscapes, multiple light sources and exotic figures, to create different moods."
- **Q8 -- National Gallery of Ireland, on Goudt's engraving (search snippet):** Elsheimer's Flight is "one of the most enchanting nocturnal landscapes in Western European art and the first ever recording of the Milky Way in paint."
- **Q9 -- Philip McCouat (Journal of Art in Society):** "Elsheimer was certainly the first painter to have the bold idea of using an objective, naturalistic and extremely detailed, comprehensive view of the heavens as the basis for an artistic work."

## Network

45 numbered entries, covering 47 persons -- N5 covers both Bril brothers and N44 covers both Krix and von Dohna. Dates are from sources checked this run unless flagged. Second-order links and unverified links are marked as such.

- **N1. Philipp Uffenbach (15 January 1566 - 6 April 1636).** Teacher. Elsheimer was apprenticed to Uffenbach in Frankfurt; Uffenbach was himself trained by Hans Grimmer. Frankfurt painter, etcher and printmaker; published the Zeitweiser sundial booklet in 1598. (EN Wikipedia "Philipp Uffenbach", read end to end, 48 lines.)
- **N2. Johann (Hans) Rottenhammer (1564 - 14 August 1625).** Employer and introducer. Rottenhammer employed Elsheimer as an assistant in Venice in 1598/99 and introduced him to Paul Bril; two of Rottenhammer's drawings belonged to Elsheimer as gifts. Rottenhammer was the first German artist to specialise in cabinet paintings on copper and collaborated with Bril, Jan Brueghel the Elder and Elsheimer. (Search results; the article title is "Hans Rottenhammer", not "Johann Rottenhammer" -- see F38.) FLAG: one drawing-gift inscription is contested -- Jacoby showed the verso inscription on catalogue no. 5 is not by Elsheimer (HNA review, search snippet).
- **N3. Friedrich Brentel (1580 - 1651).** Education link, infobox-only. EN Wikipedia "Adam Elsheimer" lists Brentel under Education, but the article body never mentions him. Brentel was a Strasbourg miniaturist and engraver whose most famous pupil was Johann Wilhelm Baur. The Strasbourg visit of 1596 is itself only "probably" in the sources. FLAG: the teacher link is infobox-only; treat as thin (see F36).
- **N4. Giovanni Faber (1574 - 17 September 1629).** Friend and collector. Papal doctor and botanist, curator of the Vatican botanical garden, member and secretary of the Accademia dei Lincei (member 29 October 1611), friend of Galileo, Rottenhammer and Elsheimer; credited with coining the word "microscope". Faber was, with Paul Bril, a witness at Elsheimer's marriage in 1606. Not a maker -- network only.
- **N5. Paul Bril (1554 - 1626) and Matthijs Bril (1550 - 1584 per the 1822 James catalogue; commonly given as d. 1583).** Friend and collaborator. Bril was a witness at Elsheimer's marriage (with Faber), painted a picture together with Elsheimer (now at Chatsworth), and was owed money at Elsheimer's death. Bril was a leading Flemish landscape painter in Rome. His brother Matthijs arrived in Rome c. 1575 and collaborated with Rottenhammer. FLAG: Bril birth 1554 (British Museum index) vs 1556 (James 1822 catalogue) vs c. 1553/54. Matthijs death 1583 vs 1584.
