Hendrik Kobell (13 September 1751, Rotterdam - 3 August 1779, Rotterdam; dates search-verified only, from EN Wikipedia via search S29). Dutch landscape and marine painter, etcher, draftsman, watercolourist; cousin of Ferdinand and Franz Kobell; father trained him in drawing, he loved drawing ships in Rotterdam port; London 1769-1770, Paris 1772, Amsterdam Stadstekenakademie; founder of the Rotterdam drawing society Hierdoor tot Hooger; father and teacher of Jan Kobell II (1778-1814); pupils Carel Frederik Bendorp and Gerrit van der Pals; The Shipwreck (1775, Rijksmuseum SK-A-1218). Dominique Michael Serres (also Dominic Michael Serres; c. 1763 - 1816; dates search-verified only, from the Government Art Collection). Younger son of Dominic Serres; landscape painter (the only Serres who left the sea); served in the Royal Navy on HMS Foudroyant 1782; married Lucretia Madden 4 August 1788; prisoner of the King's Bench Prison by December 1813; died 1816; View of the Teign, Devon (GAC). New names from Kobell's own network -- verified NOT STUDIED, for a later round (NOT part of the 8 extra spares above): Carl Rottmann (11 January 1797, Handschuhsheim - 7 July 1850, Munich; as commonly cited, not verified from a primary source). German landscape painter, most famous of the Rottmann family. First drawing lessons from his father Friedrich, who taught drawing at Heidelberg University; moved to Munich in 1821/1822, married Friederike von Sckell in 1824, and through her came to the attention of Crown Prince Ludwig, who sent him to Italy in 1826/1827 to sketch the 28 Italian landscapes in fresco for the Hofgarten arcades (completed 1833), then commissioned a Greek cycle in 1834 (23 large landscapes, now in the Neue Pinakothek). Court painter in 1841. His school included Karl Lindemann-Frommel, Karl Ludwig Seeger, August Loffler and August Seidel. WHY: the Munich landscapist who raised landscape to the height of history painting in fresco - the direct successor to Dillis's and Kobell's Munich. The doorway is: from Dillis's Munich, hop to the painter Ludwig sent to Italy to fresco a whole arcade of landscapes. VERIFIED NOT STUDIED: zero "## Study #" headers; zero whole-log mentions; no local file (parent verified both). Peter von Cornelius (23 September 1783, Dusseldorf - 6 March 1867, Berlin; as commonly cited, not verified from a primary source). German painter, a main representative of the Nazarene movement. Drawing classes at the Dusseldorf Academy from age twelve; Rome 1811-1819 with the Lukasbund; summoned to Munich by Crown Prince Ludwig in 1819 to decorate the Glyptothek; director of the Dusseldorf Academy in 1821 and of the Munich Academy from 1824/1825; his "Last Judgment" (1829-1840) fills the east wall of the Ludwigskirche in Munich, 19 m by 12 m. His pupils included Karl Hermann, Wilhelm von Kaulbach and Adam Eberle; he left Munich for Berlin in 1839-1840. WHY: the academy director who rebuilt German painting around fresco and the Munich school - the institutional successor to Kobell's generation. The doorway is: from Kobell's Munich Academy, hop to the Nazarene who took its directorship and covered a church wall with the Last Judgment. (The 14 names carried in #539 are carried forward here, NOT re-proposed as new: Jacob de Wit (bapt. 19 December 1695, Amsterdam - d. 12 November 1754, Amsterdam), Johann Heinrich Roos (29 September 1631, Otterberg - 3 October 1685, Frankfurt), Thomas Heeremans (bapt. 29 May 1641, Haarlem - bur. 24 January 1694, Haarlem) (mains); Jacob Koninck (c. 1615, Amsterdam - c. 1695) (reserve); Jacob Coning (Jacob Koninck II; c. 1647, The Hague - 16 July 1724, Copenhagen), Klaes Molenaer (Nicolaes Molenaer; 1626-1629, Haarlem - 1676, Haarlem) (alternates); spares: Aernout Smit (1641, Amsterdam - 1710), Michiel Maddersteg (bapt. 5 January 1662, Enkhuizen - d. 1708, Berlin), Jan Claesz Rietschoof (21 May 1652, Hoorn - 3 November 1719, Hoorn), Ferdinand Kobell (7 June 1740, Mannheim - 1 February 1799, Munich), Franz Kobell (23 November 1749, Mannheim - 14 January 1822, Munich), Johann Georg von Dillis (26 December 1759, Gmain - 28 September 1841, Munich), Willem Romeyn (also spelled Romeijn; c. 1624, Haarlem - 1694, Haarlem), Isaac de Jouderville (1612, Leiden - 1645, Amsterdam). Jacob de Wit (bapt. 19 December 1695, Amsterdam - d. 12 November 1754, Amsterdam; as commonly cited, not verified from a primary source) - the man who painted stone that was not there, on the ceilings of Amsterdam. Dutch Rococo painter and interior decorator; pupil of Albert van Spiers and Jacob van Hal, Antwerp Guild of St Luke 1714; copied Rubens's ceilings in the Carolus Borromeuskerk in watercolour 1711-1722, the record after lightning destroyed them in 1718 (engraved by his pupil Jan Punt, 1751); famous for door and ceiling paintings in Amsterdam canal houses (he lived on the Keizersgracht) and country houses on the Vecht; grisailles he called "witjes" - grey-white paintings faking stone relief; Moses Choosing the Seventy Elders (1737, Amsterdam Town Hall), Apotheosis of Aeneas for Pieter Pels (Herengracht 479), ceiling with Isaac de Moucheron; pupils Jan Punt, Pieter Tanje, Dionys van Nijmegen, Jan de Groot, Frans and Jacob Xavery; follower Tako Hajo Jelgersma. He is main 2 rather than top pick because he is the Moucheron line's other half - the figure painter inside the wall-painter's room - and the witje is the one trompe-l'oeil mechanism this chain has never studied. The doorway is the man who painted stone that was not there, on the ceilings of Amsterdam. FLAG: one conservation page heads him "(1685-1754)" while its own body gives baptized 19 December 1695 - the 1685 is a typo. Johann Heinrich Roos (29 September 1631, Otterberg - 3 October 1685, Frankfurt; as commonly cited, not verified from a primary source) - the German who painted Italian shepherds he never saw, and died in the fire that took them. German Baroque landscape and animal painter; family fled to Amsterdam in the Thirty Years' War (1640); trained with Guilliam du Gardijn, Cornelis de Bie and Barent Graat, but Berchem and Dujardin shaped him; worked for Ernst, Landgrave of Hesse-Rheinfels 1654-1659; court painter to Charles I Louis, Elector Palatine, 1664; moved to Frankfurt 1667; died in a house fire in 1685 trying to save his belongings. Specialised in pastoral idylls - idealized landscapes with ancient ruins, shepherds, sheep and cattle, the longing for harmony between men and animals and nature; never went to Italy, built his ruins from engravings; also one of the finest German portraitists of his time; many individual animal drawings (Albertina), intensifying the individuality of each species; Goethe called him the "Raphael of all livestock painters"; sons Philipp Peter Roos (Rosa di Tivoli, 1655-1706) and Johann Melchior Roos (1663-1731) were also painters. He is main 3 rather than top pick because he is the German pastoral before Kobell, the model Govert van der Leeuw painted "in the manner of" and one of the masters Kobell engraved after - the animal piece as an ideal, not a copy. The doorway is the German who painted Italian shepherds he never saw, and died in the fire that took them. Thomas Heeremans (bapt. 29 May 1641, Haarlem - bur. 24 January 1694, Haarlem; as commonly cited, not verified from a primary source) - the winter painter who signed his name so tightly that dealers invented a man called Mans. Dutch painter and art dealer; winter landscapes, cityscapes, harbour scenes, beach views, river views, village scenes; Haarlem Guild of St Luke 1664; may have been a pupil of Caesar van Everdingen (no influence of Everdingen visible - Heeremans was purely a landscape painter, not a figure painter); style shaped by Klaes Molenaer, but brighter in palette and livelier in movement, villagers skating, sledging, fishing and talking; frequently returned to Scheveningen beach and Egmond village, beach and ruins; pictures often go in pairs; collaborated with specialist figure painters - Abraham Storck painted the figures in Winter landscape with the Montelbaanstoren, Amsterdam; signed "THMANS" with the letters run together, so dealers long misread him as "Frederik Hendrik Mans". He is main 4 rather than top pick because he is the staffage problem from the other side: a landscapist who hired the figures in, the mirror of van de Velde the hired staffage hand. The doorway is the winter painter who signed his name so tightly that dealers invented a man called Mans. Jacob Koninck (c. 1615, Amsterdam - c. 1695; as commonly cited, not verified from a primary source) - the teacher whose perspective books made his pupil better than him. Dutch Golden Age landscape painter; lent his perspective books to Johannes Verkolje, who became better at perspective drawing than he was; teacher of his son Jacob II (Coning) and his younger brother Philips Koninck (carried #537); uncle of Salomon Koninck; in Dordrecht 1633-1636, Rotterdam 1637-1645, The Hague 1647-1651, Amsterdam 1658, Copenhagen by c.1676, painter to the court of Christian V of Denmark. He is the reserve because he is the hinge of the whole Koninck family and a second van de Velde disciple whose line runs to Copenhagen, but his own oeuvre is thin and his dates are the shakiest in this block. The doorway is the teacher whose perspective books made his pupil better than him. VERIFIED NOT STUDIED: zero "## Study #" headers; zero whole-log mentions; no local file; not carried in #522-#538. Jacob Coning (Jacob Koninck II; c. 1647, The Hague - 16 July 1724, Copenhagen; as commonly cited, not verified from a primary source) - the fellow pupil who took van de Velde's landscape to a Danish court and died in the poorhouse. Dutch-Danish painter to the Danish Court; son of Jacob Koninck; trained under his father and Adriaen van de Velde - a fellow pupil of van Bergen by the same master, and the #538 pupil list's fourth name; Copenhagen 1676, court work from 1680 (contact possibly through his wife, who worked for Queen Charlotte Amalie); one of the first painters in Denmark-Norway to specialise in topographical painting; Norway 1698-1699 for Frederiksberg Palace; with Thomas Quellinus, Hendrik Krock and others proposed an art academy in Copenhagen on 6 October 1701; also a portraitist to Danish and Norwegian nobility; despite court contact he died in the poorhouse of the Reformed Church in Store Kongensgade after repeated forced auctions. The doorway is the fellow pupil who took van de Velde's landscape to a Danish court and died in the poorhouse. Klaes Molenaer (Nicolaes Molenaer; 1626-1629, Haarlem - 1676, Haarlem; as commonly cited, not verified from a primary source) - the Haarlem winter painter whose birth was never written down because his family was Catholic. Dutch Golden Age landscape painter and draughtsman; son of tailor Jan Mienssen Molenaer, brother of Bartholomeus and Jan Miense Molenaer (carried #535 main); Roman Catholic family, so no baptismal register and no exact birth date; Haarlem Guild of St Luke 1651, dues paid yearly until 1676; winter landscape painter influenced by Jacob van Ruisdael; frozen rivers teeming with skaters, sleds, horse-drawn sleighs, a church and an inn under low winter sun; taught by the little-known Nicolaes Piemont; earliest dated work 1644; works in the Rijksmuseum, Boijmans Van Beuningen, Schwerin, Braunschweig. The doorway is the Haarlem winter painter whose birth was never written down because his family was Catholic. Aernout Smit (1641, Amsterdam - 1710; dates search-verified only, not read end to end) - the attic painter who copied Bakhuizen on the record. Marine painter; pupil of Jan Theunisz Blanckerhoff; painted in an attic room on the Singel, probably employed by dealer Laurens Cornelisz de Coninck; follower and copyist of Ludolf Bakhuizen (1687/1689 notarial records of copying Bakhuizen); The Africa in Table Bay; most marines 1667-1678, later style tending to Bakhuizen; Amsterdam Guild of St Luke 1688; died aged about 70, buried at the Leiden cemetery. Michiel Maddersteg (bapt. 5 January 1662, Enkhuizen - d. 1708, Berlin; as commonly cited, not verified from a primary source) - the marine painter who built his patron real ships. Marine painter; studied under Ludolf Bakhuizen; spent most of his life at the court of Frederick I of Prussia, returned to Amsterdam later in life, where he commissioned the building of two beautiful ships for Frederick I's pleasure; most of his paintings are in Germany. Jan Claesz Rietschoof (21 May 1652, Hoorn - 3 November 1719, Hoorn; as commonly cited, not verified from a primary source) - the Hoorn pupil who copied his master's storm the year after it was painted. Dutch Golden Age seascape painter; pupil of Abraham Liedts, then of Ludolf Bakhuizen; active in Hoorn all his career, possibly some time in Amsterdam with Bakhuizen; taught his son Hendrik; one of Bakhuizen's most accomplished pupils (Sotheby's, on a stormy sea after Bakhuizen's Fishing Vessels Offshore in a Heavy Sea, 1684, completed 1685); works in the Rijksmuseum, Het Scheepvaartmuseum, Westfries Museum Hoorn, the Hermitage, the National Maritime Museum Greenwich; Hoorn contemporary of the flower painter Jacob Rootius, son of Jan Albertsz Rotius. The doorway is the Hoorn pupil who copied his master's storm the year after it was painted. Ferdinand Kobell (7 June 1740, Mannheim - 1 February 1799, Munich; as commonly cited, not verified from a primary source) - the law student who became the etcher of the Dutch landscape in Germany. German painter and copper engraver; grandson of Johann Heinrich Kobell, grocer of Frankfurt who settled in Mannheim in 1720; studied law at Heidelberg, then painting under Peter Verschatfelt; court painter 1766; Paris 1768-1769 under Johann Georg Wille; followed Elector Charles Theodore to Munich 1794; named Director of the Palatinate Painting Gallery 1798 but died before taking up the post; landscapes in the style of Berchem; about 300 etchings, published as Oeuvres completes (1809), marking a distinct advance in German landscape etching; father of Wilhelm, brother of Franz. The doorway is the law student who became the etcher of the Dutch landscape in Germany. Franz Kobell (23 November 1749, Mannheim - 14 January 1822, Munich; as commonly cited, not verified from a primary source) - the uncle who drew twenty thousand landscapes because paint was too slow. German painter, etcher and draftsman; brother of Ferdinand, uncle and teacher of Wilhelm; sent to Italy by Elector Karl Theodor 1776, in Rome until 1785, working from nature and monumental buildings; court painter at Munich; produced only a few oil paintings, highly praised by Goethe (Rocky Landscape with Waterfalls, once in the Bamberg gallery), but left 20,000 landscape and architecture pen drawings and etchings, painting being too slow to keep pace with his ideas. The doorway is the uncle who drew twenty thousand landscapes because paint was too slow. Johann Georg von Dillis (26 December 1759, Gmain - 28 September 1841, Munich; dates search-verified only, not read end to end) - the professor Kobell replaced, who taught Munich to paint what it saw. German landscape painter, pioneer of Naturalism; trained under Johann Jakob Dorner the elder and at the Munich Zeichnungsakademie; inspector of the Hofgarten Galerie 1790; professor of landscape painting at the Munich Academy 1808-1814, replaced by Wilhelm von Kobell in 1813/1814; central gallery director 1822, founding director of what became the Alte Pinakothek; mentor of Carl Rottmann and Ludwig Richter; insisted on Naturstudium, direct study of nature, against the history-painting hierarchy. The doorway is the professor Kobell replaced, who taught Munich to paint what it saw. Willem Romeyn (also spelled Romeijn; c. 1624, Haarlem - 1694, Haarlem; dates search-verified only, not read end to end) - the Berchem pupil whose pastures Kobell was still copying two centuries later. Dutch Italianate landscape painter and printmaker; pupil of Nicolaes Berchem around 1642; Haarlem Guild of St Luke 1646; sun-drenched southern landscapes with shepherds, cattle, sheep and goats resting by rivers, long shadows, hazy golden light; last dated works 1694; Kobell etched after him (The Shepherd's Rest, 1819, after Romeyn). The doorway is the Berchem pupil whose pastures Kobell was still copying two centuries later. Isaac de Jouderville (1612, Leiden - 1645, Amsterdam; as commonly cited, not verified from a primary source) - the orphan pupil whose tronies were filed under Rembrandt for centuries. Dutch Golden Age painter, pupil of Rembrandt; orphan, parents from Metz; apprenticed to Rembrandt from 1 November 1629, six signed receipts for 1630-1631, travelled with him to Amsterdam in 1631; enrolled at Leiden University April 1632; married Maria le Febure 1636; Deventer 1641; back in Amsterdam 1643, died young in 1645; his widow married glassmaker Pieter de Melder in 1648, and his daughter Mariecke later married Frederik de Moucheron - the Jouderville line marries straight into the Moucheron line; painted mainly Rembrandtesque heads or tronies, so faithful that several of his paintings were previously attributed to Rembrandt; Man in Oriental Costume was the Fake or Fortune? case (BBC, 2011), a Nazi-seized Oppenheimer stock picture. The doorway is the orphan pupil whose tronies were filed under Rembrandt for centuries. Extra verified spares (not part of the 8 above, all verified NOT STUDIED): Jan Baptist Boel the Younger (between September and 4 October 1643, Antwerp - 9 January 1689; dates search-verified only, from EN Wikipedia via search S22). Son and pupil of Pieter Boel; family moved to Paris 1668 for the Gobelins; married Maria Catharina Immenraet, daughter of landscape painter Philips Augustijn Immenraet, 4 May 1675; collaborated with other Antwerp painters, adding the still-life and animal elements to their compositions; known oeuvre very limited; assimilated his father's style so completely that attribution problems follow (Nationalmuseum). Ludolf Bakhuizen (28 December 1630 - 7 November 1708; as commonly cited, not verified from a primary source) - the marine painter who took the sea when the van de Veldes left: clerk and calligrapher turned painter, student of Everdingen then Dubbels, who went out in an open boat to study storms and became the leading Dutch marine painter after 1672. He is main 2 rather than top pick because the hop runs through the pupil first, and Bakhuizen is the sea line, not the cattle line. Hendrick Dubbels (bapt. 2 May 1621, Oude Kerk - bur. 20 October 1707, Nieuwe Zuids chapel; as commonly cited, not verified from a primary source) - the studio man: spent most of his career working in other marine artists' studios (de Vlieger, then both van de Veldes), trained Bakhuizen and then worked for him; went bankrupt as a haberdasher-draper selling caps, bonnets and stockings; his late work is inferior replicas of his own earlier pictures. The doorway is the studio hand who taught the master and then worked for him. VERIFIED NOT STUDIED: zero "## Study #" headers; zero whole-log mentions; no local file; not carried in #520-#537. His is the study of the marine workshop that painted the whale hunt. The doorway is the workshop painter who painted Peter the Great's visit. His is the study of the apprentice who followed his master from Antwerp to Paris to Rome. The doorway is the apprentice who followed Boel to Rome and joined the Bent. His is the study of the notebook painter who let the king choose the picture. The doorway is the huntsman with a notebook whom the king chose from. (The 11 names carried in #537 are carried forward here, NOT re-proposed as new: Frans Snyders (11 November 1579 - 19 August 1657), Paul de Vos (1591/92 or 1595 - 30 June 1678) (mains); Melchior d'Hondecoeter (c. 1636 - 3 April 1695) (reserve); Willem van de Velde the Younger (bapt. 18 December 1633 - 6 April 1707) (alternates); spares: Gerrit Berckheyde (bapt. 6 June 1638 - 10 June 1698), Pieter de Molijn (bapt. 6 April 1595 - 23 March 1661), Caesar van Everdingen (1616/17 - bur. 13 October 1678), Pieter de Grebber (c. 1600 - 1652/53), Willem Cornelisz Duyster (1599-1635), Willem van de Velde the Elder (c. 1611 - 13 December 1693), Jan Fyt (bapt. 15 March 1611 - 11 September 1661). Frans Snyders (11 November 1579 - 19 August 1657; as commonly cited, not verified from a primary source) - the Antwerp root of the whole hunt and animal line. Pupil of Pieter Brueghel the Younger 1593, then Hendrick van Balen; master 1602; Italy 1608-09 (Rome, Milan, Cardinal Borromeo); married Margaretha de Vos 1611 (sister of Cornelis and Paul de Vos); Rubens collaboration from the 1610s; Torre de la Parada 1636-38, about 60 hunting and animal pieces after Rubens designs, plus 18 more 1639; dean 1628; pupils Nicasius Bernaerts, Peter van Boucle, Juriaen Jacobsze, Jan Roos, Paul de Vos; Jan Fyt student and assistant from 1629; patrons Archduke Albert VII, Philip IV of Spain, Bishop Antonius Triest; influenced Jan Davidsz. de Heem and the 18th-century French animaliers Oudry and Desportes; the concert of birds theme; died childless. He is main 3 rather than top pick because the hop runs through the son's copying, not through Snyders directly, and his scale would swamp the Haarlem chain. The doorway is the Antwerp root of the hunt. VERIFIED NOT STUDIED: zero "## Study #" headers; zero whole-log mentions; no local file; not carried in #520-#536. Dates from the EN Wikipedia article read end to end in leg 3. Paul de Vos (1591/92 or 1595 - 30 June 1678; as commonly cited, not verified from a primary source) - Snyders's brother-in-law and workshop colleague; the second Antwerp hunt painter. Pupil of Denijs van Hove 1604, David Remeeus 1605, Eduard Snayers; worked in his brother-in-law Frans Snyders' workshop (Snyders married his sister Margaretha 1611); guild master 1620; monumental hunts for aristocrats, warmer colours and broader brush than Snyders, fighting cats, horses attacked by wolves; collaborator with Rubens (Torre de la Parada), Van Dyck, Jan Wildens; patrons Marquis of Leganes and Philippe-Charles 3rd Count of Arenberg (36+ paintings 1633-1640); many works long misattributed to Snyders; never dated his paintings. He is main 4 rather than top pick because he is Snyders's shadow, and the study should meet Snyders first. The doorway is the brother-in-law in Snyders's shadow who painted the fighting cats. VERIFIED NOT STUDIED: zero "## Study #" headers; zero whole-log mentions; no local file; not carried in #520-#536. Dates search-verified from leg 3 search S10. FLAG: dates search-verified only, not from a page read end to end in this leg. Reserve: Melchior d'Hondecoeter (c. 1636 - 3 April 1695; as commonly cited, not verified from a primary source) - the bird painter: grandson of Gillis, son of Gijsbert, nephew of J. B. Weenix, cousin of Jan Weenix; trained by his father Gijsbert then his uncle J. B. Weenix; Confrerie Pictura 1659; married Susanne Tradel 1663; painted almost exclusively birds (exotic and game) in park landscapes; about 250 works, only about 20 dated; pupils Willem Hendrik Wilhelmus van Royen and Jan Weenix; employed by William III to paint his menagerie at Het Loo; his inventory lists a small gallows (to pose birds) and seven Snyders paintings; followers include Adriaen Coorte, Jan van Huysum, Elias Vonck; early sea-pieces and Tub with Fish 1655 before switching to birds. He is the reserve because his mechanism set (the bird as still life in a park landscape, the menagerie as subject) is the most specialised in the network, a strong contrast study whenever the chain wants one. The doorway is the bird painter with a gallows in his inventory. VERIFIED NOT STUDIED: zero "## Study #" headers; zero whole-log mentions; no local file; not carried in #520-#536. Dates from the EN Wikipedia article read end to end in leg 3. Alternates if any of the 4 fails later (all verified NOT STUDIED): Willem van de Velde the Younger (bapt. 18 December 1633 - 6 April 1707; as commonly cited, not verified from a primary source) - the marine line: son of Willem the Elder, elder brother of Adriaen; trained by his father then Simon de Vlieger (Weesp); Amsterdam until 1672, then England with his father, Charles II service, studio at Queen's House Greenwich, Westminster 1691; leading Dutch marine painter of the later 17th century. He is an alternate rather than a main because the marine line is a step sideways from the hunt, and his brother is the top pick. The doorway is the elder brother who painted the sea while the younger painted the cattle. Spares (verified, NOT part of the 8 below): Gerrit Berckheyde (bapt. 6 June 1638 - 10 June 1698; as commonly cited, not verified from a primary source) - Haarlem; younger brother and pupil of Job Berckheyde; Rhine trip 1650s, Heidelberg court of Charles I Louis, golden medal; Haarlem Guild 27 July 1660; RKD: Italianate landscapes, portraits, cavalry pieces; influenced by Pieter Saenredam; staffage possibly by Lingelbach and Jan van Huchtenburg; no pupils documented; influenced Jan ten Compe and Isaac Ouwater; views of the Grote Markt and Grote Kerk Haarlem, Amsterdam Town Hall, The Hague. His is the study of the Haarlem townscape painter who also painted cavalry pieces, the town answer to Oudendijck's camp. The doorway is the Haarlem townscape painter the son followed. Pieter de Molijn (bapt. 6 April 1595 London - 23 March 1661 Haarlem; as commonly cited, not verified from a primary source) - Guild Haarlem 1616, dean 1633, 1638, 1646; tonal-phase pioneer with Jan van Goyen and Salomon van Ruysdael; pupils Gerard ter Borch the Younger and Elder, Allaert van Everdingen, Jan Wils, Jan Coelenbier; collaborated with ter Borch, Frans Hals, Pieter de Grebber, Jacob de Wet, Jacob Pynas, Nicolaes Berchem; son Anthony de Molijn (1635-c. 1703). His is the study of the tonal pioneer who taught the generation before Oudendijck. The doorway is the tonal pioneer who taught ter Borch and Everdingen. Caesar van Everdingen (1616/17 - bur. 13 October 1678; as commonly cited, not verified from a primary source) - pupil of Jan Gerritsz van Bronckhorst (carried #529); guild Alkmaar 1632; Haarlem 1648 (guild and schutterij); Oranjezaal, Huis ten Bosch, with Jacob van Campen (carried #530); older brother of Allaert (carried #534) and Jan van Everdingen; Haarlem classicist. His is the study of the classicist brother in a landscape family. The doorway is the classicist brother of the waterfall painter. His is the study of the classicist son who never married and painted seventy pictures. The doorway is the classicist son of Rubens's Haarlem agent. Willem Cornelisz Duyster (1599-1635; as commonly cited, not verified from a primary source) - Amsterdam; with his friend Pieter Codde developed and popularised the guardroom scene (cortegaerdje); married Margrieta Kick 1631, cousin of Jan and Salomon de Bray (both carried); died of plague 1635; quarrel with Codde 1625; small oeuvre, only three dated works. His is the study of the guardroom painter who died of plague at thirty-six - and the man whose Tric-trac Players are still confused with Dusart's (see C475 of #536). The doorway is the guardroom painter whose name still causes confusion. VERIFIED NOT STUDIED: zero "## Study #" headers; zero whole-log mentions; no local file; not carried in #520-#536. Willem van de Velde the Elder (c. 1611 - 13 December 1693; as commonly cited, not verified from a primary source) - pen paintings (penschilderij) in black ink on vellum, panel and canvas; sailed with the fleet sketching battles (Scheveningen 1653, Sole Bay 1672, about 70 sketches); Charles II paid him 100 pounds a year for "draughts of sea fights" and the same to his son to colour them; father of Willem the Younger and Adriaen, and daughter Magdalena (b. 1632); Amsterdam studio on Nieuwe Waalseiland; commissions from Admiral Cornelis Tromp; died London 1693, buried St James's Piccadilly. His is the study of the father who drew sea battles in pen while his sons painted them in oil. The doorway is the father who sketched the fleet in pen. VERIFIED NOT STUDIED: zero "## Study #" headers; zero whole-log mentions; no local file; not carried in #520-#536. Dates search-verified from leg 3 search S24. FLAG: dates search-verified only. Jan Fyt (bapt. 15 March 1611 - 11 September 1661; as commonly cited, not verified from a primary source) - Snyders's pupil and assistant 1629-31, master 1629/30; Italy 1633-41 (Paris, Venice for the Sagredo and Contarini families, Rome 1635, Bentvueghels bent-name "Goudvink", bullfinch); back in Antwerp by 1641; Guild of Romanists dean 1652; married Francoise van de Sande 22 March 1654, four children; about 380 paintings (287 extant still lifes per Christie's); moved the dead-game display from the indoor table to the open landscape, the first to paint game as a trophy of the hunt rather than food; pupils Pieter Boel and Jacob van der Kerckhoven; collaborators Cornelius Schut, Thomas Willeboirts Bosschaert, Erasmus Quellinus II; Set of the Dogs 1642 dedicated to Don Carlo Guasco, Marquess of Soleno. His is the study of the man who took dead game out of the kitchen and into the landscape. The doorway is the pupil who moved the game from the table to the field. Extra verified spares (not part of the 8 above, all verified NOT STUDIED): Hendrick de Meijer (1620-1689; as commonly cited, not verified from a primary source) - Rotterdam; RKD: landscape painter of the "Albert Cuyp school" (Cuyp carried #533); specialist in large-scale military and encampment scenes, low horizons, silvery skies - the closest subject match to Evert's own soldiers resting outside encampments; works in the Rijksmuseum and the Louvre; Departure of Charles II from Scheveningen, 2 June 1660; active Rotterdam 1646-1689 per Christie's. VERIFIED NOT STUDIED: zero "## Study #" headers; zero whole-log mentions; no local file; not carried in #520-#536. Dates search-verified from leg 3 search S25. FLAG: dates search-verified only; not to be confused with the later Hendrik Meijer (1744-1793); artsdot and wikioo wrongly attribute the 1660 Departure to the later Meijer. Philips Koninck (5 November 1619 - 4 October 1688; as commonly cited, not verified from a primary source) - Amsterdam; younger brother and pupil of Jacob Koninck; Houbraken says he completed his training in Rembrandt's studio (Britannica doubts it); from the late 1640s the panoramic landscapes, canvas split half earth and half sky, bands of shade and light after Rembrandt's etchings and Hercules Segers; figures by Adriaen van de Velde in Woodland border and countryside; about 300 drawings; owned a barge line Amsterdam-Rotterdam via Leiden; stopped painting after 1676. VERIFIED NOT STUDIED: zero "## Study #" headers; zero whole-log mentions; no local file; not carried in #520-#536. Dates search-verified from leg 3 search S27. FLAG: dates search-verified only; the Stadel gives birth 15 November 1619 against the commonly cited 5 November. Jan van der Heyden (5 March 1637 - 28 March 1712; as commonly cited, not verified from a primary source) - Gorinchem-Amsterdam; one of the first Dutch painters to specialise in townscapes and a leading architectural painter of the Golden Age; his most frequent collaborator was Adriaen van de Velde; designed and implemented Amsterdam's street-lighting scheme (1669-1840, adopted abroad); married Sara ter Hiel 1661; 14 paintings connected with Maarssen, probably for Joan Huydecoper II; died wealthy, over 70 of his own paintings in his estate; Room Corner with Curiosities painted at 75 in 1712, the year he died. VERIFIED NOT STUDIED: zero "## Study #" headers; zero whole-log mentions; no local file; not carried in #520-#536. Dates search-verified from leg 3 search S29. FLAG: dates search-verified only. Simon de Vlieger (c. 1601 Rotterdam - bur. 13 March 1653 Weesp; as commonly cited, not verified from a primary source) - marine painter; Delft Guild 1634; early work after Jan Porcellis (his teacher per the Royal Museums Greenwich), then his own silvery light and cool palette; teacher of Willem van de Velde the Younger (c. 1648-1650) and, per one low-quality source, of Adriaen van de Velde; the bridge between the second and third generations of Dutch marine painters; also influenced Hendrick Dubbels and Jan van de Cappelle. VERIFIED NOT STUDIED: zero "## Study #" headers; zero whole-log mentions; no local file; not carried in #520-#536. Dates search-verified from leg 3 search S30. FLAG: dates search-verified only; one low-quality page (kalden) reverses the teacher line and calls him Willem van de Velde's student; the weight of sources (Thyssen, Christie's, Royal Museums Greenwich, Houbraken) has him as the teacher. Jan Porcellis (1580/84 Ghent - 29 January 1632 Zoeterwoude; as commonly cited, not verified from a primary source) - marine painter; initiated the decisive transition from early realism to the tonal phase: overcast skies, rough waters, low horizons, two-thirds of the canvas sea and sky; supposed pupil of Hendrick Vroom (per Houbraken); Antwerp guild 1617, Rotterdam by 1615, Haarlem around 1622; teacher of Simon de Vlieger per the Royal Museums Greenwich; the most influential marine painter of the first half of the 17th century; only about ten works securely identified. VERIFIED NOT STUDIED: zero "## Study #" headers; zero whole-log mentions; no local file; not carried in #520-#536. Dates search-verified from leg 3 search S31. FLAG: dates search-verified only; birth uncertain, 1580 or 1584. Hercules Segers (c. 1589 - c. 1638; as commonly cited, not verified from a primary source) - Haarlem-born; pupil of Gillis van Coninxloo; the most inspired, experimental and original landscapist of his period and an even more innovative printmaker; 54 plates, 183 known impressions, about 15 paintings; printed in coloured ink on coloured paper and cloth, hand-coloured, cropped to different sizes, each impression a miniature painting; invented lift-ground (sugar-bite) etching, not used again for 150 years; Rembrandt owned eight of his paintings and a printing plate; art dealer by trade; wife recorded as a widow in 1638. Pieter Codde (11 December 1599 - 12 October 1678; as commonly cited, not verified from a primary source) - Amsterdam; with his friend Willem Duyster developed and popularised the guardroom scene (cortegaerdje); the 1625 jar fight with Duyster at Van Someren's estate ended in bloodshed; commissioned 1637 to complete Hals's unfinished schutterstuk, the Meagre Company; pupil Willem Duyster (about the same age); Jacob Duck influenced by him; Simon Kick brother-in-law; most prolific and influential genre painter in Holland during the 1630s per Steigrad/Rosen. (The 14 names carried in #536 are carried forward here, NOT re-proposed as new: Salomon de Bray (1597 - 11 May 1664), Leendert van der Cooghen (9 May 1632 - 18 February 1681), Dirk Maas (12 September 1659 - 25 December 1717) (mains); Jan van Huchtenburg (bapt. 20 November 1647 - bur. 2 July 1733) (reserve); Jan Wyck (29 October 1645 - 17 May 1702), Jacob Gole (1660-1737) (alternates); spares: Egbert van Heemskerck the Elder (1634 - c. 1702/04), Evert Collier (26 January 1642 - few days before 8 September 1708), Adriaen Oudendijck (1677-1704), Jacob Jordaens (19 May 1593 - 18 October 1678), Rachel Ruysch (3 June 1664 - 12 October 1750), Jan van Gool (1685-1763), Gerrit Claesz Bleker (1592 - 8 February 1656), John Wootton (c. 1682 - 13 November 1764). Salomon de Bray (1597 - 11 May 1664; as commonly cited, not verified from a primary source) - the draftsman whose figure studies Dusart kept: they sit in Dusart's 1704 probate inventory alongside Bega's 125 figure studies, so Dusart the collector valued de Bray's drawing as highly as his own master's circle. He is also the Haarlem classicist against whom the whole peasant line defines itself: history painter, architect of the City Hall and the Zijlpoort, town planner, poet, designer of silverwork, author of Architectura Moderna (1631), headman of the Guild of St. Luke who tried and failed to rewrite its charter in 1631 to put painting above the crafts. He died of plague in 1664 with four of his children; his sons Jan, Dirck and Joseph carried the name on, Jan already carried as a #535 spare. He is main 2 rather than top pick because he predates Dusart by a generation and the hop runs through the probate inventory, not through a shared master. The doorway is the classicist whose figure studies Dusart kept in his own inventory. VERIFIED NOT STUDIED: zero "## Study #" headers; zero whole-log mentions; no local file; not carried in #520-#535. Dates from the EN Wikipedia article read end to end in leg 3. Leendert van der Cooghen (9 May 1632 - 18 February 1681; as commonly cited, not verified from a primary source) - the other draftsman in Dusart's probate inventory, and the closest parallel to Dusart himself as a draughtsman: an amateur of independent means who never had to sell, about 66 drawings and only 3 certain paintings, figure and portrait studies in red and black chalk drawn directly from the life model, dated day-month-year on 46 sheets so a chronology can be built, moving from a static dry style to a looser freer one. He trained with Jacob Jordaens, shared a studio with Cornelis Bega, befriended Vincent van der Vinne, and never married - "uncle Leendert" of the Schagelstraat, the bachelor counterpart to Dusart the bachelor who lived with his aunts. His mechanism set (the dated single-figure chalk study, the amateur who draws only when he feels like it) is the direct mirror of Dusart's own single-peasant chalks. The doorway is the amateur whose dated chalk studies Dusart collected and whose life mirrors his own. VERIFIED NOT STUDIED: zero "## Study #" headers; zero whole-log mentions; no local file; not carried in #520-#535. Dates from the EN Wikipedia article read end to end in leg 3. Dirk Maas (12 September 1659 - 25 December 1717; as commonly cited, not verified from a primary source) - the pupil-chain hop with a direct Dusart document behind it: he was back in Haarlem in May 1692 on the testimony of Cornelis Dusart himself (Bredius), and he is the painter who took the Haarlem line to war - pupil of Mommers, then Berchem, then Jan van Huchtenburg, then painter of the Battle of the Boyne first-hand in 1690 for William III and for his patron Hans Willem Bentinck, Earl of Portland. He also did staffage for other painters and worked in the same studio as Adriaen Oudendijck 1695-1700, so he ties the Oudendijck top pick to the Huchtenburg reserve and the Wyck alternate in one knot. The doorway is the horse painter Dusart himself testified for, who painted the Boyne first-hand. FLAG: Gerson gives 1656-1717. Reserve: Jan van Huchtenburg (bapt. 20 November 1647 - bur. 2 July 1733; as commonly cited, not verified from a primary source) - the battle machine of the network: first taught by Thomas Wijck, then trained in Paris under Adam Frans van der Meulen at the Gobelins, then court painter to Prince Eugene of Savoy with a ten-canvas series of Eugene's battles from Zenta (1697) to Belgrade (1717), plus William III's campaigns. He taught Dirk Maas, collaborated with Jan Wyck, assisted Gerrit Berckheyde by painting his people and horses, and his pupils Alexander van Gaelen and Izaak Vogelensanck carried the Wouwerman battle style into England. He is the reserve because his mechanism set (the monumental multi-figure battle composition, the court-painter campaign series) is the largest scale in the network, a strong contrast study whenever the chain wants one. The doorway is the court painter who turned ten of Prince Eugene's battles into one series. Alternates if any of the 4 fails later (all verified NOT STUDIED): Jan Wyck (29 October 1645 - 17 May 1702; as commonly cited, not verified from a primary source) - the son of Thomas Wijck who carried the Haarlem line to England and founded British military and sporting painting: over 150 works, the Battle of the Boyne (1690) painted half a dozen times, William III's campaigns through the Nine Years' War, and as a teacher the master of John Wootton and Martin Beckman. He accompanied Dirk Maas to Ireland to paint William's campaigns, and his battle pieces bring the viewer into the fight at eye level instead of the prevailing bird's-eye disposition of troops. He is an alternate rather than a main because the date conflict needs settling before he can be a top pick. The doorway is the Haarlem battle painter who founded British sporting art from Mortlake. FLAG: dates badly conflicted - infobox 29 October 1645, body 29 October 1652, Gerson 1652-1700, ODNB c. 1645-1700, Government Art Collection c. 1640-1702; the 1676 marriage certificate ("about 31") supports c. 1645, which is used here. Jacob Gole (1660-1737; as commonly cited, not verified from a primary source) - the print hop: he worked with Dusart, became an early practitioner of mezzotint, published Dusart's own mezzotints (the Communia Gaudia series including The Patriotic Peasant, c. 1695) in Amsterdam, and engraved the portrait of Adriaen van Ostade after Dusart - so he is the man who carried both Dusart and Ostade into print. His series of the Five Senses, the Seven Virtues, the Greek and Roman goddesses and the Seven Continents give a study a printmaking mechanism set (mezzotint tone, the publisher's series) that no painter in the chain so far has offered. The doorway is the engraver who published Dusart's mezzotints and engraved Ostade after him. Spares (verified, NOT part of the 7 below): Egbert van Heemskerck the Elder (1634 - c. 1702/04; as commonly cited, not verified from a primary source) - the Haarlem genre painter who took the Ostade-Brouwer low life to London and turned it into satire: taverns, peasant cottages, surgical theatres, backgammon players, Quaker meetings, under the patronage of John Wilmot, 2nd Earl of Rochester. Thought to have trained with Jan Miense Molenaer (already carried as a #535 main), travelled to Italy 1655-1661, and is constantly confused with his son of the same name. His is the study of the peasant tavern become English satire. The doorway is the Haarlem satirist who took the tavern to London and Rochester. Evert Collier (26 January 1642 - few days before 8 September 1708; as commonly cited, not verified from a primary source) - the vanitas and trompe-l'oeil painter of the Haarlem guild register: probably pupil of Vincent van der Vinne, guild 1664 (the same impossible-1646 register page as Oudendijck and Heemskerck), then Leiden, Amsterdam and London from 1693. His letter racks, skulls, pocket watches, globes and hourglasses are the still-life answer to Dusart's peasant: the same Haarlem, the opposite subject, and the same London emigration as Wyck and Heemskerck. The doorway is the letter-rack painter who shared Oudendijck's impossible guild date. Adriaen Oudendijck (1677, Haarlem - 1704, Haarlem; as commonly cited, not verified from a primary source) - the copycat son: second son of Evert, he copied his cattle after Adriaen van de Velde and his figures after Thomas Wyck, earning the nickname "Rapianus" from Houbraken; worked with Dirk Maas, possibly in the same studio, 1695-1700; guild 1700; died in 1704, the same year as Dusart. He is the study of second-handedness at one remove from Dusart's own copying of Ostade - what it means to build a career out of other men's cattle and figures. The doorway is the copycat who died the same year as Dusart. VERIFIED NOT STUDIED: zero "## Study #" headers; zero whole-log mentions; no local file; not carried in #520-#535. Jacob Jordaens (19 May 1593 - 18 October 1678; as commonly cited, not verified from a primary source) - the Flemish giant behind van der Cooghen: pupil of Adam van Noort (his only teacher, whose daughter he married), never went to Italy, the third of the Antwerp trio after Rubens and Van Dyck, painter of history, genre and tapestry cartoons, collaborator on the Oranjezaal at Huis ten Bosch where van der Cooghen may have trained with him. He is the biggest name in the spares and the farthest step sideways; he is a spare, not a main, because the hop runs through a single pupil (van der Cooghen) and his scale would swamp the Haarlem chain. The doorway is the Antwerp master who taught the amateur in Dusart's inventory. Rachel Ruysch (3 June 1664 - 12 October 1750; as commonly cited, not verified from a primary source) - the flower painter of the network: apprenticed at 15 to Willem van Aelst until his death in 1683, married the portraitist Juriaen Pool in 1693, ten children, court painter to the Elector Palatine Johann Wilhelm, still painting at 83. Her link to Dusart runs through Michiel van Musscher (already carried as a #535 alternate), who painted her 1692 portrait in collaboration with her - she painted the flowers, he painted her at work in her studio. She is the second woman in the chain after Judith Leyster, and the first whose career outlasted the whole Haarlem generation by half a century. The doorway is the flower painter Musscher painted at work in her own studio. VERIFIED NOT STUDIED: zero "## Study #" headers; zero whole-log mentions; no local file; not carried in #520-#535. Jan van Gool (1685-1763; as commonly cited, not verified from a primary source) - the biographer who wrote Dusart's life: pupil of Simon van der Does and Mattheus Terwesten, Hague Drawing School director 1720-1734, author of the Nieuwe Schouburg (1750) as an update to Houbraken, with Dusart on p. 457 and the note that his folios were "appreciated by art collectors who conserved them very carefully". A study of van Gool would be a study of how Dusart was remembered, parallel to the Houbraken spare in #535 - and of the Hague Italianate landscape he painted himself. The doorway is the biographer who wrote Dusart's life and kept his folios in mind. VERIFIED NOT STUDIED: zero "## Study #" headers; zero whole-log mentions; no local file; not carried in #520-#535. Gerrit Claesz Bleker (1592 - 8 February 1656; as commonly cited, not verified from a primary source) - the Haarlem landscape painter of historical allegories: guild before 1643, probably pupil of Nicolaes Moeyaert, teacher of David Decker (whose only known work was long attributed to Cornelis Decker, already carried as a #535 spare) and of Dirck Bleker, and thought to have taught the young Paulus Potter in the mid-1630s. He is the landscape answer to Dusart's cottage interior, one generation up. The doorway is the Haarlem allegorist who may have taught the young Paulus Potter. John Wootton (c. 1682 - 13 November 1764; as commonly cited, not verified from a primary source) - the English end of the Wyck line: apprenticed to Jan Wyck in the late 1690s at Mortlake, then the first English painter of horse portraits and the founder of English sporting painting at Newmarket, with patrons from Queen Anne to the Duke of Marlborough and Sir Robert Walpole. His hunting scenes carry Wyck's influence more than his horse portraits do, and he influenced George Stubbs and Thomas Gainsborough. He is the study of a Dutch import become an English institution. The doorway is the page boy who became the father of English sporting art. VERIFIED NOT STUDIED: zero "## Study #" headers; zero whole-log mentions; no local file; not carried in #520-#535. (The 14 names carried in #535 are carried forward here, NOT re-proposed as new: Frans Hals (c. 1582 - 26 August 1666), Jan Miense Molenaer (1610 - buried 19 September 1668), Judith Leyster (bapt. 28 July 1609 - 10 February 1660) (mains); Dirck Hals (19 March 1591 - 17 May 1656) (reserve); Richard Brakenburg (bapt. 22 May 1650 - 28 December 1702), Michiel van Musscher (bapt. 27 January 1645 - 20 June 1705) (alternates); spares: Joos van Craesbeeck (c. 1605/06 - c. 1660), Hendrik Mommers (bapt. 2 January 1620 - buried 21 December 1693), Jacob Duck (c. 1600 - buried 28 January 1667), Cornelis Gerritsz Decker (1618-1678), Frans Pietersz de Grebber (1573-1649), Johannes Cornelisz Verspronck (1597-1662), Jan de Bray (d. 1697). Frans Hals (c. 1582 - 26 August 1666; as commonly cited, not verified from a primary source) - Ostade's teacher and the root of the whole Haarlem line: the loose, visible brushstroke, the fleeting expression, the merry company, the tronie. He taught or shaped Ostade, Brouwer, Molenaer, Leyster, Verspronck, Roestraten and van der Vinne, and his five painter sons carried the name into the next generation. His late monochrome regent pieces and his 19th-century rehabilitation (Thore-Burger, the Impressionists, van Gogh's praise) give a study a second arc beyond the teaching link. He is main 2 rather than top pick because the chain has just arrived at Ostade from below (the Boths, Bloemaert) and Dusart continues it forward, while Hals is a step back up the tree. The doorway is the teacher whose loose brush Ostade learned from and then left behind. VERIFIED NOT STUDIED: zero "## Study #" headers; zero whole-log mentions of the exact phrase "Frans Hals"; no local file; not carried in #520-#534. Jan Miense Molenaer (1610 - buried 19 September 1668; as commonly cited, not verified from a primary source) - the fellow pupil who shared Hals's studio with Ostade and then shared a studio and a marriage with Judith Leyster, and the hinge between Hals and Jan Steen: his style is called a precursor to Steen's, his early work is close to Hals and his later work close to Ostade, and his Five Senses series of 1637 is the most overt visual humour of the Dutch Golden Age. He also brings the literary strand (proverbs and poems by Jacob Cats and Bredero) that Ostade never used. The doorway is the fellow pupil who married the other fellow pupil and became the forerunner of Jan Steen. VERIFIED NOT STUDIED: zero "## Study #" headers; zero whole-log mentions; no local file; not carried in #520-#534. FLAG: the Hals article's influence list gives his birth as 1609; his own article gives 1610. Judith Leyster (bapt. 28 July 1609 - 10 February 1660; as commonly cited, not verified from a primary source) - the woman of the Hals circle: possibly taught by Frans Pietersz de Grebber, possibly a pupil of Hals, a Guild member at 24 (1633), running her own workshop with pupils, suing Hals himself over a stolen apprentice, then marrying Molenaer in 1636. Her entire oeuvre - only eighteen works attributed with certainty, nearly all from 1628-1635 - was filed under Hals's or her husband's name for close to 200 years until Hofstede de Groot's rediscovery in 1893, when a "Frans Hals" in the Louvre turned out to be signed with her JL-star monogram. She is the strongest non-pupil hop from Ostade and the only woman in the immediate network. The doorway is the woman of the Hals circle whose whole oeuvre was filed under her husband's name for two centuries. Reserve: Dirck Hals (19 March 1591 - 17 May 1656; as commonly cited, not verified from a primary source) - Frans's younger brother and the inventor, with Willem Buytewech and Esaias van de Velde, of the merry-company picture: small, colourful, fashionable young people at music, cards and conversation, painted from about 1618-1620 to the end of his life, with a late Leiden period (1641-1648) and a collaboration line with Dirck van Delen. He influenced Nathaniel Bacon, Johann Hulsman and Willem Cornelisz Duyster, and his son Anthonie carried the name on. He is the reserve because his mechanism set (the polite merry company) is the mirror image of Ostade's peasant tavern, which makes him a strong contrast study whenever the chain wants one. The doorway is the younger brother who turned the merry company into a genre of its own. Alternates if any of the 4 fails later (all verified NOT STUDIED): Richard Brakenburg (bapt. 22 May 1650 - 28 December 1702; as commonly cited, not verified from a primary source) - the pupil who imitated the wrong master: taught by Ostade (and Hendrik Mommers) but, per Hofstede de Groot, he imitated Jan Steen more closely than either teacher, in style, character types and the arrangement of weddings, merrymakings and quack-doctors, with figures repeated over and over and little invention of his own. His pupil Hendrik de Valk had less still. He is the study of second-handedness itself: what happens when a painter copies the copyist, and where repetition stops being a style. The doorway is the pupil who copied Steen more faithfully than his own master. VERIFIED NOT STUDIED: zero "## Study #" headers; zero whole-log mentions; no local file; not carried in #520-#534. Michiel van Musscher (bapt. 27 January 1645 - 20 June 1705; as commonly cited, not verified from a primary source) - the pupil with the most documented education in the network: he kept his own record - two months with Martinus Saeghmolen (1660), Abraham van den Tempel (1661), seven lessons with Gabriel Metsu (1665), three months with Ostade in Haarlem (1667) - then became Amsterdam's sought-after portraitist, painting Tsar Peter the Great and the Russian Great Embassy of 1697, and teaching Ottmar Elliger the Younger and Dirk Valkenburg. He is the study of the pupil who outgrew the peasant genre entirely and took Ostade's characterisation into high portraiture. The doorway is the pupil who took seven lessons from Metsu, three months from Ostade, and became Amsterdam's portraitist. His pupils Adriaen Rombouts and Lucas Viters extend the chain one more step. The doorway is the baker of the Antwerp citadel who learned to paint from a prisoner. Hendrik Mommers (bapt. 2 January 1620 - buried 21 December 1693; as commonly cited, not verified from a primary source) - the Haarlem painter of vegetable market scenes who was Brakenburg's other teacher, Dirk Maas's first teacher, a follower of Nicolaes Berchem and an Italy traveller with the Bentvueghels, linking Ostade's Haarlem to the Italianate landscape line that Berchem carried. The doorway is the Haarlem market painter who taught Dirk Maas and travelled to Italy with Berchem. Jacob Duck (c. 1600 - buried 28 January 1667; as commonly cited, not verified from a primary source) - the goldsmith turned painter: master goldsmith in Utrecht in 1619, drawing lessons from Droochsloot from 1621, active in Haarlem 1636-1646 alongside Ostade, and the central painter of the guardroom (kortegaardje) and the brothel scene, blending Utrecht Caravaggist humour with Haarlem merry-company painting. His is the study of the soldier's tavern where Ostade painted the peasant's. The doorway is the goldsmith turned guardroom painter who learned drawing from Droochsloot. He is the landscape answer to Ostade's cottage interior. The doorway is the Haarlem landscapist who painted in the manner of Ruisdael and shared Ostade's streets. Johannes Cornelisz Verspronck (1597-1662; as commonly cited, not verified from a primary source) - the neat portraitist of Haarlem: son of the portraitist Cornelis Engelsz, one of about ten competing Haarlem portraitists, possibly a pupil of Hals, and the representative of the "neat" school against which Hals's loose painterly style is defined in the Hals article's opening. Pieter van Laer (1599 - c. 1642; as commonly cited, not verified from a primary source) - called Il Bamboccio, the Rome contemporary Hendrick almost certainly brushed against: settled in Rome on the Via Margutta in 1625, the year before Hendrick was registered there, and turned Caravaggesque chiaroscuro away from saints and card-players and onto beggars, peddlers and mule-drivers in the Campagna - the bambocciate, taken up by Andries Both, Jan Both and Jan Asselijn. His bent-name in the Schildersbent was "de Snuffelaar". The doorway is the man who took the candlelight down to the street. The doorway is the successor who ran the same guild a generation later. (The 13 names carried in #531 are carried forward here, NOT re-proposed as new: Cornelis Bloemaert II (1603 - 28 September 1692; as commonly cited, not verified from a primary source) - second son, painter turned engraver; studied with his father, his brothers Hendrick and Adriaen, and Gerard van Honthorst; learned printmaking from Crispijn van de Passe the Elder. Paris 1630 (engravings for Michel de Marolles's Temple des Muses), Rome from 1633 until his death - 59 years, most of his career. Bentvueghels bent-name "Winter". His Roman workshop attracted young engravers from abroad and spread Italian art through Europe; best-known engravings after Annibale Carracci (The Holy Family), Pietro da Cortona (Adoration of the Shepherds) and Rubens (Meleager); 1664-77 he worked on the engraved record of Cortona's Palazzo Pitti frescoes. Pupils Michel Natalis and Gilles Rousselet. He delayed returning to see his dying father so long that his father died - he stayed in Rome. The doorway is the son who went: the father never went to Italy and learned Caravaggism second-hand - now follow the son who did go, stayed 59 years, and engraved Italy for the rest of Europe. VERIFIED NOT STUDIED: zero "## Study #" headers; zero whole-log mentions; no local file; not carried in #520-#530. Death date flagged as disputed: Wikipedia gives 28 September 1692; the NGA gives "in or after 1684" and Te Papa "c. 1684"; an older cyclopedia says 1680. 1692 is the commonly cited date. Leonaert Bramer (24 December 1596 - buried 10 February 1674; as commonly cited, not verified from a primary source) - Delft painter of genre, religious and history scenes, listed by the RKD among Bloemaert's pupils. At 18 he left on a long trip reaching Rome in 1616 via Arras, Amiens, Paris, Aix, Marseille, Genoa and Livorno; in Rome he was a founder of the Bentvueghels (bent-name "Nestelg...") and lived with Wouter Crabeth, once got into a fight with Claude Lorrain, and acquired the sobriquet "Leonardo della Notte" - Leonardo of the Night - for his nocturnes. He met Wybrand de Geest in Aix-en-Provence in 1616. Back in Delft by 1628, Guild of St. Luke 1629; painted frescoes, a rarity north of the Alps (not survived), and murals on canvas. A lifelong bachelor, productive until his death at 77; it is possible, though undocumented, that Vermeer trained with him. One of the most intriguing personalities in 17th-century Dutch art. The doorway is the Delft pupil who ran off to Rome at eighteen, helped found the Bentvueghels, fought with Claude Lorrain, and came home to paint the night. VERIFIED NOT STUDIED: zero "## Study #" headers; zero whole-log mentions; no local file; not carried in #520-#530. Jan Baptist Weenix (1621-1659; Leiden Collection gives 1660/61 - death date disputed, flag it; as commonly cited, not verified from a primary source) - painter of the Dutch Golden Age, Bloemaert pupil (after Jan Micker, before Claes Moeyaert). Born near Amsterdam harbour, son of an architect; travelled to Italy 1643-47, joined the Bentvueghels as "Ratel" (rattle, for a speech defect), worked for Cardinal Camillo Pamphili (later Pope Innocent X), painted a portrait of Rene Descartes, and in 1649 became master of the Utrecht Guild of St. Luke alongside Jan Both as officer. Mainly responsible for introducing the Italian harbour scene into Dutch art - mid-size paintings with a group of figures in the foreground - alongside Italian landscapes with large figures among ruins, seaside views, and late still lifes of dead game and dogs. With Berchem and Jan Both, a leader of the second generation of Dutch Italianate painters; teacher of his son Jan Weenix and nephew Melchior d'Hondecoeter. Died at 39 at Huis ter Mey, Vleuten. The doorway is the Utrecht pupil who took the master's Italianate landscape and turned it into the Dutch harbour scene - and died at thirty-nine, leaving a son who became the great still-life painter. VERIFIED NOT STUDIED: zero "## Study #" headers; zero whole-log mentions; no local file; not carried in #520-#530. (Mains 2, 3 and 4; main 1 is the top pick above.) Reserve: Wybrand Simonsz. de Geest (16 August 1592 - c. 1661; as commonly cited, not verified from a primary source) - Frisian portrait painter, the most important portraitist of Friesland. Learned first from his father Simon Juckesz, a stained-glass worker, then studied with Bloemaert in Utrecht. Grand Tour 1614-18 through France and Italy; met Leonaert Bramer in Aix-en-Provence in 1616; in Rome joined the Bentvueghels as "De Friesche Adelaar" (the Frisian Eagle) and kept an album amicorum (friendship book) from 1611, 22 drawings from which are still missing. Returned to Leeuwarden c. 1620 and became the leading portrait painter of the Frisian nobility and the Orange-Nassau family; married Hendrickje Fransdr Uylenburgh in 1622, niece of Saskia van Uylenburgh - Rembrandt's wife - linking him by marriage to Rembrandt's circle; influenced Jacob Adriaensz Backer; students Jan Jansz. de Stomme and Jacob Potma; sons Julius and Frank also became painters. The doorway is the Frisian pupil who carried Utrecht portraiture north to Leeuwarden, married into Rembrandt's family, and signed his Roman years "the Frisian Eagle". VERIFIED NOT STUDIED: zero "## Study #" headers; zero whole-log mentions; no local file; not carried in #520-#530. Alternates if any of the 5 fails later (all verified NOT STUDIED): Jacob Gerritsz. Cuyp (1594-1651/52; as commonly cited, not verified from a primary source) - Dordrecht portrait and landscape painter, RKD-listed pupil of Bloemaert; son of stained-glass designer Gerrit Gerritsz. Cuyp, half-brother of Benjamin Gerritsz. Cuyp, father of the far more famous Aelbert Cuyp. Joined the Dordrecht Guild of St. Luke 18 July 1617, treasurer 1629/33/37/41; helped found the Dordrecht painters' breakaway guild in 1642 and is called the nestor of the Dordrecht painters. Best known today for portraits - especially fine portraits of children - and pastoral landscapes; early work shows distinct Caravaggesque influence from his Utrecht training. Teacher of Ferdinand Bol, Aelbert Cuyp, Benjamin Cuyp, Paulus Lesire, Aert van der Neer and Pieter Hermansz Verelst; Jacob's recommendation is said to have sent Nicolaes Maes and Ferdinand Bol to Rembrandt. The doorway is the Utrecht pupil who went home to Dordrecht, founded its painting school, and taught the father of the golden light - Aelbert Cuyp's father, and Rembrandt's Bol. VERIFIED NOT STUDIED: zero "## Study #" headers; zero whole-log mentions; no local file; not carried in #520-#530. Birth year flagged: 1594 is the current consensus against 1575 in the 9th edition of the Britannica - the old date is wrong, use 1594. Nicolaus Knupfer (1609 - 15 October 1655; as commonly cited, not verified from a primary source) - Dutch Golden Age painter, born Leipzig, apprenticed there to Emanuel Nysen, then Magdeburg making brushes for artists until 1630, when he moved to Utrecht to work with Bloemaert - living with him for two years - before setting up his own Utrecht studio (visiting member of the Guild of St. Luke, 1637). Painted small-scale biblical, literary and mythological scenes and popular genre works; worked on the decorations of Kronborg Castle in Denmark; painted the figures in the landscapes of Jan Both and Jan Baptist Weenix - the staffage hand inside two other Bloemaert pupils' pictures. A successful teacher: his students included Jan Steen, Gabriel Metsu, Ary de Vois and Pieter Crijnse Volmarijn. The doorway is the Leipzig brush-maker who lived two years in the master's house, then painted the little figures inside Jan Both's and Weenix's landscapes - and taught Jan Steen. VERIFIED NOT STUDIED: zero "## Study #" headers; zero whole-log mentions; no local file; not carried in #520-#530. Spares (verified, NOT part of the 7): Hieronymus Francken I (c. 1540-1610; as commonly cited, not verified from a primary source) - Flemish painter, pupil of Frans Floris, worked at Fontainebleau 1566-72, court painter to the French court from 1594; Bloemaert's Paris/Fontainebleau master 1581-83. Elegant Mannerist groups of dancers, musicians and courtiers, uniting Antwerp, Venetian and French elements. Brother of Frans Francken I and Ambrosius Francken I; father of a painting dynasty. The doorway is the Fontainebleau master who gave the boy from Gorinchem the only real training he ever got. VERIFIED NOT STUDIED: zero "## Study #" headers; zero whole-log mentions; no local file; not carried in #520-#530. Adriaen Bloemaert (after 1609 - buried 8 January 1666; as commonly cited, not verified from a primary source) - fourth son, first instruction from his father; Italy until 1637, then Vienna, Salzburg (employed there), Freistadt 1640, back in Utrecht by 1651; painted history and portraits "with some success". THIN RECORD WARNING: few works securely attributed; most biographical detail comes from Houbraken/Bryan via the RKD. The doorway is the youngest-but-one son who took the family trade to Vienna and Salzburg and came home to die in Utrecht. VERIFIED NOT STUDIED: zero "## Study #" headers; zero whole-log mentions; no local file; not carried in #520-#530. Frederick Bloemaert (c. 1614/17-1690; as commonly cited, not verified from a primary source) - youngest artist son, engraver; published his father's Artes Apellae liber (1650) - the drawing book that taught Utrecht to draw into the 19th century. Dates as commonly cited (c. 1614/17) - flag as approximate. THIN RECORD WARNING: known chiefly as his father's publisher/engraver rather than for independent work. The doorway is the son who turned his father's drawings into the textbook every Utrecht artist learned from. VERIFIED NOT STUDIED: zero "## Study #" headers; zero whole-log mentions; no local file; not carried in #520-#530. Andries Both (1612/13 - 23 March 1642; as commonly cited, not verified from a primary source) - elder brother of Jan Both, Bloemaert pupil; Rouen 1633, Rome from 1635, shared a studio with Jan van Causteren, then lived with his brother on the Via Vittoria from 1638; a leading bambocciante - low-life Roman genre scenes after Pieter van Laer, influenced by Brueghel and Adriaen Brouwer - while his brother painted idyllic landscapes. Drowned in a Venetian canal in 1642, aged 29 or 30, on the way home. The doorway is the elder Both brother, who painted Rome's low life while his brother painted its golden light - and drowned on the way home. VERIFIED NOT STUDIED: zero "## Study #" headers; zero whole-log mentions; no local file; not carried in #520-#530. Cornelis de Beer (dates unknown) - RKD-listed Bloemaert pupil; son of Joos de Beer (Bloemaert's own second teacher) - the teacher's son becoming the pupil's pupil. THIN RECORD WARNING: no dates or secure works surfaced in this leg's searches; propose only if a deeper dig finds material. The doorway is the son of Bloemaert's own teacher, who came back to learn from the boy his father once taught. VERIFIED NOT STUDIED: zero "## Study #" headers; zero whole-log mentions; no local file; not carried in #520-#530. Gerrit Splinter (dates unknown) and Joos de Beer (active 1575-91) - Bloemaert's first two Utrecht teachers - Splinter the drunken dauber who lasted two weeks, de Beer "not the best of painters". THIN RECORD WARNING: both are known almost entirely through van Mander's anecdotes; no independent oeuvre to study. List as a pair only; either alone is too thin for a full study. The doorway is the two bad masters - the drunk and the mediocrity - whose failure sent a fifteen-year-old to Paris. VERIFIED NOT STUDIED: zero "## Study #" headers; zero whole-log mentions each; no local files; not carried in #520-#530.)

(The 25 names carried in #530 are carried forward here, NOT re-proposed as new: Jusepe de Ribera (Xativa, baptised 17 February 1591 - Naples 1652; as commonly cited, not verified from a primary source) - Spanish painter, "Lo Spagnoletto", the chief visual source for Baburen's saints. Cussida commissioned Ribera's Five Senses in 1615-16 while he was commissioning Baburen's Pieta Chapel, and Franits makes Ribera the chief visual source for Baburen's Archimedes, St. Francis and Pan Holding a Syrinx - the hard, sculptural, plebeian body that Baburen brought home to Utrecht. Ribera then did what Baburen never lived to do: he stayed, married into Neapolitan painting, ran the city for thirty years under the viceroys, etched forty plates, and pushed tenebrism to its most brutal extreme in martyrdoms and flayings. The doorway is the Spaniard who shared Baburen's patron and taught him his saints. VERIFIED NOT STUDIED: zero "## Study #" headers; zero whole-log mentions; no local file; not carried in #520-#529. WARNING: death date disputed (2 September vs 3 November 1652); Ribalta pupillage undocumented, Giovanni Serodine (Ascona 1600 - Rome 21 December 1630; as commonly cited, not verified from a primary source) - Ticinese Caravaggist in Rome. Serodine's Saint Michael was painted for San Pietro in Montorio, the same church where Baburen and de Haen decorated the Pieta Chapel, and his Christ among the Doctors (Louvre) and Jesus and the Tribute Money (National Gallery of Scotland) are the most violently brushed tenebrist canvases of the 1620s Roman scene. He was the son of Ticinese stucco workers, trained by his elder brother Giacomo, made few friends and patrons in Rome (Baglione), produced only about fifteen surviving paintings, and died at 30 - the same short fuse as Baburen, the same city, the same decade, the opposite temperament: loose, luminous, provincial, eccentric where Baburen is monumental and composed. Revived by Roberto Longhi; a 2022 dossier exhibition in Modena centred on his Santo scrivente. The doorway is the Caravaggist Baburen never met but shared a church with. VERIFIED NOT STUDIED: zero "## Study #" headers; zero whole-log mentions; no local file; not carried in #520-#529, Johannes Vermeer (Delft, baptised 31 October 1632 - 15 December 1675; as commonly cited, not verified from a primary source) - Dutch painter, the long echo of the Procuress. Baburen's Procuress hung in Maria Thins's house, and Vermeer painted it into the background of The Concert (c. 1664) and Lady Seated at a Virginal (c. 1670), and may have taken his own Procuress (1656) from it - the one object, the Essential Vermeer site notes, that materially links us to Vermeer's world besides his own 37 paintings. A Vermeer study after Baburen is the long echo: the brothel scene Baburen staged as a loud, cropped, candlelit transaction becomes, forty years later, a small framed picture on a quiet wall, its moral noise turned into pictorial meaning. The doorway is the painting on the wall. VERIFIED NOT STUDIED: zero "## Study #" headers; one whole-log mention only, inside the Baburen material; no local file; not carried in #520-#529. NOTE FOR THE PARENT: Vermeer is the biggest name in this list; hold or promote him at the parent's discretion. Reserve: Bartolomeo Cavarozzi (Viterbo, born 15 February 1587 - Rome 21 September 1625; as commonly cited, not verified from a primary source) - Italian Caravaggist who carried the style to Spain the way Baburen carried it to Utrecht. Taken into the Palazzo Crescenzi before 1617 by his patron Giovanni Battista Crescenzi, he travelled with him to Madrid in 1617, worked on the Escorial, and returned to Rome in 1619 - the exact years Baburen was painting the Pieta Chapel a few streets away. His work is the lyrical, still-life-haunted side of the movement (Virgin and Child, Sacrifice of Isaac, Saint Jerome with Two Angels, 1617), recently revalued after decades of neglect; Gianni Papi's 2015 monograph is the first and only one as of 2019. The doorway is the Italian who carried Caravaggism to Spain the way Baburen carried it to Utrecht. VERIFIED NOT STUDIED: zero "## Study #" headers; zero whole-log mentions; no local file; not carried in #520-#529. WARNING: birth year sometimes given as 1586; the Guercino-training claim is an error (Cavarozzi was four years older than Guercino). Alternates if any of the 4 fails later (all verified NOT STUDIED): Herman van Swanevelt (Woerden 1603 - Paris 1655; as commonly cited, not verified from a primary source) - Dutch pastoral landscapist, Bentvueghel (bent-name "heremiet", the hermit) who reached Rome in 1629. Baburen's Granida and Daifilo (1623) was the first painting of Hooft's play and started the Dutch pastoral vogue; Swanevelt made the pastoral landscape itself the subject - sunlit contrejours, shepherds at a ford, no biblical or mythological excuse at all - and with Pieter van Laer and Claude Lorrain brought the Italianate landscape into its classical phase. He was Claude's lodger-companion and model, and the intermediary through whom Claude's art reached the north. The doorway is the pastoral after Baburen's pastoral. VERIFIED NOT STUDIED: zero "## Study #" headers; zero whole-log mentions; no local file; not carried in #520-#529. WARNING: teacher uncertain (a new hypothesis says Willem Buytewech), Claude Lorrain, born Claude Gellee (Chamagne, Duchy of Lorraine, c. 1600 or 1604/5 - Rome 23 November 1682; as commonly cited, not verified from a primary source) - French landscapist, the sun to Baburen's candle. Claude arrived in Rome around 1617, ground colours for Agostino Tassi, studied under Goffredo Wals in Naples, and by the late 1630s was the leading landscapist in Italy - the one painter of the Roman scene who put the sun itself, low and streaming, into the picture. He learned from Elsheimer's foliage and Breenbergh's example, sketched the Campagna with Poussin, and recorded every finished painting in his Liber Veritatis (British Museum) as an anti-forgery ledger. The doorway is the sun to Baburen's candle. VERIFIED NOT STUDIED: zero "## Study #" headers; zero whole-log mentions; no local file; not carried in #520-#529. WARNING: birth year disputed: his tombstone says 1600, contemporary sources say c. 1604/05. NOTE: Claude is a very large subject; he works as an alternate precisely because the lane may want the smaller Swanevelt first. Spares (verified, NOT part of the 8): Gerard Douffet (Liege 1594 - 1660/61) - the Liege Caravaggist who knew Baburen in Rome and ended his career in Utrecht. Hold: his ground overlaps Valentin and Manfredi; use if a Liege turn opens. Paulus Bor (Amersfoort c. 1601 - 10 August 1669) - Bentvueghels founder with Baburen ("Orlando"); returned 1626, decorated Honselaarsdijk with Jacob van Campen; his style drifted from Caravaggism to a strange, primitive classicism; friends drank a barrel of wine at his funeral. Hold: better held for a Bentvueghels-family follow-on. Bartholomeus Breenbergh (Deventer, baptised 13 November 1598 - Amsterdam, buried 5 October 1657) - Bentvueghels founder ("het fret"); Rome 1619-1630 with Paul Bril; influenced Claude Lorrain; teachers Pieter Lastman and Jacob Symonsz Pynas. Hold: his fame is landscape, a step sideways from the candlelight line. Orazio Borgianni (Rome 6 April 1574 - 14 January 1616) - Roman Caravaggist, Spanish years 1598-1605, principal patron the Spanish ambassador Francisco Ruiz de Castro; friend of Carlo Saraceni and Antiveduto Grammatica; accused with Saraceni of complicity in an assassination attempt on Giovanni Baglione allegedly planned by Caravaggio; threw a bottle of varnish at Caravaggio in the street. Monographic exhibition Palazzo Barberini 2020. Hold: Italian batch with Saraceni and Gentileschi. Antiveduto Grammatica (Rome 1569 - April 1626) - the head-painter ("gran Capocciante") in whose studio the young Caravaggio worked on arrival in Rome; shared patrons Del Monte, Giustiniani and the Mattei with him; Principe of the Accademia di San Luca 1624, disgraced for trying to sell its Raphael altarpiece. Hold: Italian batch. WARNING: birth given as Siena 1571 in older sources; Petrucci (Paragone, 2004) established Rome 1569. Pieter Lastman (Amsterdam 1583 - buried 4 April 1633) - the man who taught Rembrandt; in Rome 1602-1607, friend of Elsheimer and Rubens; his history paintings (David and Uriah, Coriolanus) laid the foundation of Amsterdam history painting. His pupils included Rembrandt, Jan Lievens and Breenbergh. Hold: big figure, needs a full study slot of its own; doorway runs through Elsheimer. Paul Bril (Antwerp 1553 or 1554 - Rome 7 October 1626) - the Flemish landscape patriarch of Rome; papal commissions from Gregory XIII and Clement VIII; friend of Elsheimer; teacher of Agostino Tassi; precursor of Poelenburgh, Breenbergh and Claude. Principe of the Accademia di San Luca 1620. Hold: landscape line, pair with Breenbergh. Claude Vignon (Tours 19 May 1593 - Paris 10 May 1670) - French Caravaggist; in Rome c. 1609-1617 with Simon Vouet and Valentin de Boulogne; absorbed Manfredi, Fetti and Elsheimer; later worked for Richelieu and Louis XIII; admitted to the Academie royale 1651. Hold: French batch with Valentin. Giovanni Lanfranco (Parma 26 January 1582 - Rome 30 November 1647) - Carracci pupil (Agostino, then Annibale); dome of Sant'Andrea della Valle (1625-27) and the Gesu Nuovo and San Gennaro chapel in Naples; the illusionistic ceiling painter who carried Correggio's dome language into the High Baroque. Rivalry with Domenichino, Reni and Albani for Roman patronage. Hold: ceiling-painting turn. Roelant Savery (Kortrijk 1576 - buried Utrecht 25 February 1639) - court painter to Rudolf II in Prague, with access to the imperial zoo; the most prolific painter of the dodo (ten depictions, 1611-1628) and a pioneer of the independent flower still life; returned to Utrecht 1618, died bankrupt. Nephew Hans (Jan) Savery continued the dodo pictures. Hold: his fame is natural history, a step sideways from the candlelight line. Cornelis Saftleven (Gorinchem c. 1607 - Rotterdam 1 June 1681) - the versatile younger Utrechter: farmhouse interiors, hell scenes, animal satires, proverb illustrations; van Dyck drew his portrait for the Iconographia as "Hollandus Pictor Noctium Phantasmatum" (Dutch painter of nightly and fantastic scenes); Rubens owned eight of his works. Hold: a generation younger; use for a later-Utrecht turn. Jan Mijtens, also Johannes Mytens (The Hague c. 1614 - 24 December 1670) - the Hague portraitist who carried the Granida line forward: his Meeting of Granida and Daifilo (c. 1655-60, Rijksmuseum) is one of 32 known Dutch paintings of the subject; among the leading Hague portraitists with Honthorst, Adriaen Hanneman and Pieter Nason; co-founder of the Confrerie Pictura (1656). Hold: portrait line, pair with Hanneman. NAME TRAP: Houbraken confused him with the Brussels-born Johannes Meyssens. Jacob Adriaensz. Backer (Harlingen 1608 - Amsterdam 27 August 1651) - Amsterdam's "Rembrandt's opposite": about 140 paintings in twenty years, pioneer of Amsterdam classicism, Granida and Daifilo c. 1635 (Hermitage) and the huge Crowning of Mirtillo (1641); the supposed Rembrandt pupillage is a myth debunked by Blankert and Broos. Hold: Amsterdam classicism turn. NAME TRAP: not Jacob de Backer (c. 1540-c. 1591), the Flemish Mannerist. Giuseppe Vermiglio (Milan c. 1587 - Turin, after 1635) - Milanese Caravaggist; exhibited beside Serodine in the 2022 Modena dossier show; dates and career thinly documented - thin-evidence flag, propose only if the lane wants a deep-cut Roman. Bertholet Flemalle (1614-1675; dates NOT verified in this leg - flag before use) - Douffet's only known pupil; carried the Liege Caravaggist line a generation on. Hold: thin record; pair with Douffet. Daniel Mijtens (Delft c. 1590 - The Hague 1647/48; dates NOT verified in this leg - flag before use) - uncle of Jan Mijtens, portrait painter at the Stuart court in London; the Mijtens family thread. Hold: family follow-on after Jan Mijtens. Jacob van Campen (Haarlem 1596 - Amersfoort 1657; dates NOT verified in this leg - flag before use) - architect-painter, Bor's collaborator at Honselaarsdijk; the classicising turn after Caravaggism. Hold: architecture turn.
